Automorphic Gluing
Dario Beraldo and Lin Chen

Abstract
. We prove a gluing theorem on the automorphic side of the geometric Langlands correspondence: roughly speaking, we show that the difference between DMod ⁡ ( Bun G ) \DMod(\Bun_{G}) and its full subcategory DMod ⁡ ( Bun G ) temp \DMod(\Bun_{G})^{\mathrm{temp}} of tempered objects is compensated by the categories DMod ⁡ ( Bun M ) temp \DMod(\Bun_{M})^{\mathrm{temp}} for all standard Levi subgroups M ⊊ G M\subsetneq G . This theorem is designed to match exactly with the spectral gluing theorem, an analogous result occurring on the other side of the geometric Langlands conjecture. Along the way, we state and prove several facts that might be of independent interest. For instance, for any parabolic P ⊆ G P\subseteq G , we show that the functors CT P , ∗ : DMod ⁡ ( Bun G ) → DMod ⁡ ( Bun M ) \CT_{P,*}:\DMod(\Bun_{G})\to\DMod(\Bun_{M}) and Eis P , ∗ : DMod ⁡ ( Bun M ) → DMod ⁡ ( Bun G ) \Eis_{P,*}:\DMod(\Bun_{M})\to\DMod(\Bun_{G}) preserve tempered objects, whereas the standard Eisenstein functor Eis P , ! \Eis_{P,!} preserves anti-tempered objects.



1.
Introduction

This paper is a contribution to the geometric Langlands program: we formulate and prove a gluing theorem occurring on the automorphic side of the geometric Langlands conjecture. We believe this theorem is important for at least three different reasons:

•

it is a direct geometric analogue of Langlands’ classification theorem for representations of
G(\mathbb{R})
;

•

it bypasses the usage of the
extended Whittaker category
: the latter is a main ingredient in Gaitsgory’s outline of the proof of geometric Langlands, but it has been proven to be hard to deal with beyond the cases of
G=GL_{n}
and
G=PGL_{n}
;

•

it is designed to match precisely with the
spectral gluing theorem
, the gluing theorem occurring on the other side of the geometric Langlands duality.
As far as we are aware, the only way to construct a functor that connects the two sides of the duality employs these two gluing theorems in an essential way.

Moreover, some of our techniques are quite general (second adjunctions, Weyl combinatorics, the semi-infinite category, miraculous parabolic duality,
\ldots
) and we expect them to become useful in other contexts.

1.1.
Rough statement of the main result

Let
G
be a connected reductive group and
X
a smooth projective curve, both defined over an algebraically closed ground field
k
of characteristic zero. The automorphic side of the geometric Langlands conjecture is
\DMod(\Bun_{G})
, the differential graded (DG) category of D-modules on the stack
\Bun_{G}
of
G
-bundles on
X
.
It turns out that
\DMod(\Bun_{G})
contains a remarkable full subcategory: the subcategory
\DMod(\Bun_{G})^{\mathrm{temp}}
of tempered objects. Tempered objects were introduced in
[
AG15
, Section 12]
and studied in
[
Ber21c
]
,
[
Ber21b
]
,
[
FR21
]
.

Example 1.1.1
.

The general definition of
\DMod(\Bun_{G})^{\mathrm{temp}}
will be given later: there are several different ways to present it, but they all involve the Hecke action as a common ingredient. The case of
G
of semisimple rank one is an exception: for instance, an object
{\mathcal{F}}\in\DMod(\Bun_{SL_{2}})
is tempered iff its cohomology with compact supports vanishes, see
[
Ber21b
]
. The “only if” direction is true for general
G
by
[
Ber21c
]
.

Our
automorphic gluing theorem
explains how
\DMod(\Bun_{G})
is built out of tempered objects for
G
and for its Levi subgroups.
Very roughly and heuristically, we may write

(1)

\DMod(\Bun_{G})\simeq\underset{P\in\Par}{\Glue}\,\DMod(\Bun_{M})^{\mathrm{temp}},

where
\Par
denotes the poset of standard parabolics subgroups of
G
(relative to a Borel subgroup
B
fixed once and for all) and
M=\operatorname{Levi}(P)
. Of course, all the technicalities and subtleties of the theorem are hidden in the symbol “
\Glue
”. We will come back to them later, in Section
1.4
.

Remark 1.1.2
.

Here we see the resemblance between the above statement and the description of
G(\mathbb{R})
-representations due to Langlands.

1.2.
Geometric Langlands

Now, let us recall the statement of the geometric Langlands conjecture and explain the point where the automorphic theorem will be exploited.

Consider the Langlands dual group
{\check{G}}
and the stack
{\LS_{\check{G}}}
of
{\check{G}}
-local systems on
X
. This stack is derived in general and mildly singular (quasi-smooth).
The spectral side of the geometric Langlands conjecture is the DG category
\IndCoh_{\check{\mathcal{N}}}({\LS_{\check{G}}})
of
ind-coherent sheaf with nilpotent singular support
on
{\LS_{\check{G}}}
.

In their ground-breaking work
[
AG15
]
, Arinkin and Gaitsgory defined
\IndCoh_{\check{\mathcal{N}}}({\LS_{\check{G}}})
and argued that

(2)

\IndCoh_{\check{\mathcal{N}}}({\LS_{\check{G}}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle?}}{{\simeq}}\DMod(\Bun_{G})

is a plausible formulation of the geometric Langlands conjecture. This formulation of the conjecture prompts the definition of tempered D-modules: by construction,
\IndCoh_{\check{\mathcal{N}}}({\LS_{\check{G}}})
contains the more familiar
\QCoh({\LS_{\check{G}}})
as a full subcategory, and such a subcategory ought to correspond to
\DMod(\Bun_{G})^{\mathrm{temp}}
under geometric Langlands.

In a second break-through (
[
AG18
]
), Arinkin and Gaitsgory showed that
\IndCoh_{\check{\mathcal{N}}}({\LS_{\check{G}}})
can be embedded fully faithfully into a DG category glued out of
\QCoh({\LS_{\check{M}}})
, where
{\check{M}}
runs thorugh all standard Levi subgroups of
{\check{G}}
(including
{\check{G}}
itself):

(3)

\IndCoh_{\check{\mathcal{N}}}({\LS_{\check{G}}})\hookrightarrow\underset{P\in\Par}{\Glue^{\prime}}\,\QCoh({\LS_{\check{M}}}).

Again, the technicalities are all hidden in the meaning of the symbol “
\Glue^{\prime}
”.
However, this type of gluing is not optimal, in that it does not yield an equivalence but only a fully faithful functor.

Building up on
[
AG18
]
, in
[
Ber20
]
, the first author reglued the DG categories on the RHS of (
3
) to obtain an equivalence. Roughly, the main theorem of
[
Ber20
]
yields an equivalence

(4)

\IndCoh_{\check{\mathcal{N}}}({\LS_{\check{G}}})\simeq\underset{P\in\Par}{\Glue}\,\QCoh({\LS_{\check{M}}}).

1.2.1.

We can now explain the importance of (
1
) vis-a-vis (
4
).
Namely, we are not aware of a direct way to write a plausible functor between
\IndCoh_{\check{\mathcal{N}}}({\LS_{\check{G}}})
and
\DMod(\Bun_{G})
. On the other hand, thanks to the
vanishing theorem
of
[
Gai10
]
, there is an evident functor

\mathbb{L}_{G}^{\mathrm{temp}}:\QCoh({\LS_{\check{G}}})\longrightarrow\DMod(\Bun_{G})^{\mathrm{temp}}

which ought to be an equivalence.

Remark 1.2.2
.

In more detail,
\mathbb{L}_{G}^{\mathrm{temp}}
is defined as follows. The vanishing theorem states that
\QCoh({\LS_{\check{G}}})
acts on
\DMod(\Bun_{G})
preserving
\DMod(\Bun_{G})^{\mathrm{temp}}
; we set
\mathbb{L}_{G}^{\mathrm{temp}}
to be the result of this action on
\Poinc_{!}
, a canonical tempered object of
\DMod(\Bun_{G})
.

The next step of our program (to be performed in another publication) is to prove that the functors
\mathbb{L}_{M}^{\mathrm{temp}}
, for all Levi subgroups
M\subseteq G
, assemble together to yield a functor

\underset{P\in\Par}{\Glue}\,\QCoh({\LS_{\check{M}}})\longrightarrow\underset{P\in\Par}{\Glue}\,\DMod(\Bun_{M})^{\mathrm{temp}},

that is, a functor
\mathbb{L}_{G}
as desired. It will follow from this construction that
\mathbb{L}_{G}
is an equivalence if and only if so is
\mathbb{L}_{M}^{\mathrm{temp}}
, for any Levi subgroup
M
.

In other words, our automorphic gluing theorem allows to reduce the geometric Langlands conjecture to the more concrete (but still very hard)
tempered Langlands conjecture

\QCoh({\LS_{\check{G}}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle?}}{{\simeq}}\DMod(\Bun_{G})^{\mathrm{temp}}
.

1.3.
Bypassing the Whittaker categories

In
[
Gai15b
]
, Gaitsgory outlined a strategy to prove the geometric Langlands conjecture. Let us recall this strategy and highlight the role played by our theorem.

In a nutshell, Gaitsgory’s strategy amounts to embed both sides of (
2
) into a larger DG category and compare the essential images. This larger DG category is the extended Whittaker category
\Whit(G,\mathrm{ext})
, whose definition we now recall.

It is easier to start with a function-theoretic version. We assume here that
G
has connected center.
Let
\ch_{G}
be the vector space of (additive) characters on
N({\mathbf{A}})/N(F)
. We first replace
\DMod(\Bun_{G})
with the vector space of functions
\Fun(G(F)\backslash G({\mathbf{A}})/G({\mathbf{O}}))
. Then we consider the vector space of
extended Whittaker functions

\Fun(G({\mathbf{A}})/G({\mathbf{O}})\times\ch_{G})^{T(F)\ltimes N({\mathbf{A}}),ev}

consisting of functions on
G({\mathbf{A}})/G({\mathbf{O}})\times\ch_{G}
satisfying:

•

f(n\cdot g,\chi)=\chi(n)\cdot f(g,\chi)
for all
n\in N({\mathbf{A}})
;

•

f(t\cdot g,\Ad_{t}(\chi))=f(g,\chi)
for all
t\in T(F)
.

There is an evident operator

\coeff_{G,\mathrm{ext}}:\Fun(G(F)\backslash G({\mathbf{A}})/G({\mathbf{O}}))\longrightarrow\Fun(G({\mathbf{A}})/G({\mathbf{O}})\times\ch_{G})^{T(F)\ltimes N({\mathbf{A}}),\ev}.

Moreover,
\Fun(G({\mathbf{A}})/G({\mathbf{O}})\times\ch_{G})^{T(F)\ltimes N({\mathbf{A}}),\ev}
splits into components parametrized by the elements of the coordinate stratification of
\ch_{G}
.

The standard trick with the mirabolic subgroup shows that
\coeff_{G,\mathrm{ext}}
is injective when
G=GL_{n}
and
G=PGL_{n}
. In these two cases, we have therefore embedded
\Fun(G(F)\backslash G({\mathbf{A}})/G({\mathbf{O}}))
into a simpler vector space. In fact, in spite of the long-winded definition,
\Fun(G({\mathbf{A}})/G({\mathbf{O}})\times\ch_{G})^{T(F)\ltimes N({\mathbf{A}}),\ev}
is simpler than
\Fun(G(F)\backslash G({\mathbf{A}})/G({\mathbf{O}}))
: its definition only involves
abelian
global objects, namely
\ch_{G}
and
T(F)
.

The above constructions render to the setting of geometric Langlands as follows (see
[
Ber19
]
for details):

•

there is a DG category
\Whit(G,\mathrm{ext})
, equipped with a functor

\coeff_{G,\mathrm{ext}}:\DMod(\Bun_{G})\longrightarrow\Whit(G,\mathrm{ext});

•

\Whit(G,\mathrm{ext})
naturally decomposes as a
lax limit
of the
partial Whittaker categories

\Whit(G,\mathrm{ext})\simeq\mathbin{\mathop{\operatorname{laxlim}}\displaylimits_{P\in\Par}\,}{\Whit(G,P)};

•

for
G=GL_{n}
and
G=PGL_{n}
, the functor
\coeff_{G,\mathrm{ext}}
is fully faithful (the proof uses again the mirabolic subgroup, this time combined with a blow-up construction).

Let us now summarize Gaitsgory’s strategy as presented in
[
Gai15b
]
: assuming Langlands duality for the Levi subgroups of
G
, one can construct a diagonal fully faithful arrow in the diagram below:

(5)

\textstyle{\IndCoh_{\check{\mathcal{N}}}({\LS_{\check{G}}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
[spectral gluing]
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\DMod(\Bun_{G})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\coeff_{G,\mathrm{ext}}}
\textstyle{\Glue_{P}\QCoh({\LS_{\check{M}}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\subseteq}
\textstyle{\Whit(G,\mathrm{ext})}

Now, for
G=GL_{n}
and
G=PGL_{n}
, the right vertical arrow is fully faithful too, so one can construct a geometric Langlands equivalence by checking that the essential images of the two functors into
\Whit(G,\mathrm{ext})
agree.
Gaitsgory then proposed the following statement (if true, then the same method as above would prove the geometric Langlands conjecture):

Conjecture 1.3.1

(Gaitsgory)
.

The functor
\coeff_{G,\mathrm{ext}}
is fully faithful for any reductive group
G
.

This conjecture remains open at the moment. Besides this fundamental issue, working with
\Whit(G,\mathrm{ext})
is not ideal for other (less serious) reasons:

•

when the center of
G
is disconnected, the definition must be modified and it becomes more complicated;

•

as mentioned,
\Whit(G,\mathrm{ext})
is only a lax-limit of the partial Whittaker categories, while having an actual limit would be desirable;

•

we are not aware of a DG category that precisely matches
\Whit(G,\mathrm{ext})
on the spectral side (for this reason, the bottom arrow in our previous diagram is slanted).

For all these reasons, we propose to bypass
\Whit(G,\mathrm{ext})
altogether. It can be shown that
\coeff_{G,\mathrm{ext}}
naturally factors through the automorphic gluing DG category, thereby improving the symmetry of (
5
):

(6)

\textstyle{\IndCoh_{\check{\mathcal{N}}}({\LS_{\check{G}}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
[spectral gluing]
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\DMod(\Bun_{G})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
[automorphic gluing]
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\Glue_{P}\QCoh({\LS_{\check{M}}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\subseteq}
\textstyle{\Glue_{P\in\Par}\DMod(\Bun_{M})^{\mathrm{temp}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Whit(G,\mathrm{ext})}

1.4.
Construction of the glued category

Let us now be more precise on how the automorphic gluing theorem is formulated. We will see that our rough statement (
1
) must be modified in two ways: first, the components of the gluing category must be made
\Sph_{G}
-linear; second, we must express their functoriality via twisted arrows of parabolics.

As mentioned, in general the tempered condition defining the full subcategory
\DMod(\Bun_{G})^{\mathrm{temp}}\subseteq\DMod(\Bun_{G})
is expressed in terms of the Hecke action, that is, the action of the spherical monoidal DG category
\Sph_{G}
on
\DMod(\Bun_{G})
.

1
1

1

This action requires the choice of
x\in X
and
{\mathcal{O}}\simeq{\mathcal{O}}_{x}
: as proven in
[
FR21
]
, any such choice yields the same full subcategory.

Accordingly, we postulate that all the categories appearing in the automorphic gluing theorem must be equipped with an action of
\Sph_{G}
. Likewise, all connecting functors appearing in the theorem must be
\Sph_{G}
-linear.

Remark 1.4.1
.

In general, we define
{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathrm{temp}}
for any DG category
{\mathcal{C}}
equipped with an action of
\Sph_{G}
. By definition,
{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathrm{temp}}\subseteq{\mathcal{C}}
is a full subcategory. Its right orthogonal is called the full subcategory of
anti-tempered objects
and denoted by
{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathrm{atemp}}
. See Section
2.1
for more details.

Here we encounter our first modification: for
P\subseteq G
a proper parabolic subgroup with Levi quotient
M
, the DG categories
\DMod(\Bun_{M})
and
\DMod(\Bun_{M})^{\mathrm{temp}}
carry action of
\Sph_{M}
, but not actions of
\Sph_{G}
. There is a canonical way to obtain
\Sph_{G}
-linear DG categories from these two, and it leads us to
{\mathcal{I}}(G,P)
and
{\mathcal{W}}(G,P)
respectively.

The definition goes as follows (full details will be provided in the section on preliminaries). Following
[
Bar12
,
Che20a
]
, consider the prestack
\Bun_{G}^{P{\mathrm{-gen}}}
parametrized
G
-bundles on
X
with a generic reduction to
P
.
The DG category
\DMod(\Bun_{G}^{P{\mathrm{-gen}}})
is well-defined and equipped with a natural action of
\Sph_{G}
.

Remark 1.4.2
.

Similarly to our description of the Whittaker category,
\Bun_{G}^{P{\mathrm{-gen}}}
is a geometric analogue of
P(F)\backslash G({\mathcal{A}})/G({\mathcal{O}})
and
\DMod(\Bun_{G}^{P{\mathrm{-gen}}})
is analogue to the vector space
\Fun(P(F)\backslash G({\mathcal{A}})/G({\mathcal{O}}))
.

Considering the obvious maps

(7)

\Bun_{M}\xleftarrow{q}\Bun_{P}\xrightarrow{i_{P}}\Bun_{G}^{P{\mathrm{-gen}}},

we let
{\mathcal{I}}(G,P)
and
{\mathcal{W}}(G,P)
be the DG full subcategories of
\DMod(\Bun_{G}^{P{\mathrm{-gen}}})
defined by the following fiber diagrams:

\textstyle{{\mathcal{W}}(G,P)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{I}}(G,P)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\DMod(\Bun_{G}^{P{\mathrm{-gen}}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{P}^{!}}
\textstyle{\DMod(\Bun_{M})^{\mathrm{temp}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\DMod(\Bun_{M})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q^{!}}
\textstyle{\DMod(\Bun_{P})}

It is easy to see that
\DMod(\Bun_{G}^{P{\mathrm{-gen}}})
is equipped with an action of
\Sph_{G}
.
However, it is not at all clear from the above definition that the DG categories
{\mathcal{I}}(G,P)
and
{\mathcal{W}}(G,P)
admit an action of
\Sph_{G}
. This is one of our first technical result, proven in Proposition
2.3.15
:

Proposition 1.4.3
.

The natural
\Sph_{G}
-action on
\DMod(\Bun_{G}^{P{\mathrm{-gen}}})
preserves
{\mathcal{I}}(G,P)
and
{\mathcal{W}}(G,P)
.

Remark 1.4.4
.

We can compare with the situation on the spectral side: there we see that
\QCoh({\LS_{\check{M}}})
and
\IndCoh_{\check{\mathcal{N}}_{M}}({\LS_{\check{M}}})
carry actions of
{\mathbb{H}}({\LS_{\check{M}}})
but not actions of
{\mathbb{H}}({\LS_{\check{G}}})
. Using
{\check{P}}
, there is a canonical way to produce two
{\mathbb{H}}({\LS_{\check{G}}})
-linear DG categories; for details, see the introduction of
[
Ber20
]
.

Remark 1.4.5
.

This comparison brings up a recurring theme: while the spectral gluing theorem involves the
formal geometry
of stacks of local systems, the automorphic theorem involves the
rational geometry
of the stack of bundles.

The DG categories
{\mathcal{W}}(G,P)
are the
\Sph_{G}
-linear categories that replace the
\DMod(\Bun_{M})^{\mathrm{temp}}
in our very preliminary statement automorphic gluing theorem. Thus, our rough statement (
1
) now reads

(8)

\DMod(\Bun_{G})\simeq\underset{P\in\Par}{\Glue}\,{\mathcal{W}}(G,P),

but once again the symbol
\Glue
carried a vague meaning, which we are going to clarify next. This will be our second and last modification, alluded to at the beginning of this section.

To arrive at a precise statement, we need to investigate how the components
{\mathcal{W}}(G,P)
and
{\mathcal{W}}(G,Q)
are related for
P\supseteq Q
.
The key idea is to realize that the assignment
P\mapsto{\mathcal{W}}(G,P)
can be extended to a functor out of twisted arrows of parabolics. Namely, for any inclusion
P\supseteq Q
, we define a DG category
{\mathcal{W}}(G,P\supseteq Q)
as follows. Let
M
denote the Levi quotient(/subgroup) of
P
and consider the following natural diagram:
2
2

2

That this diagram generalizes (
7
): the latter is a special case of the former for
P=Q
.

(9)

\Bun_{M}^{Q\cap M{\mathrm{-gen}}}\xleftarrow{}\Bun_{P}^{Q{\mathrm{-gen}}}\xrightarrow{}\Bun_{G}^{Q{\mathrm{-gen}}}.

Pull-push along this diagram yields a functor

\DMod(\Bun_{G}^{Q{\mathrm{-gen}}})\longrightarrow\DMod(\Bun_{M}^{Q\cap M{\mathrm{-gen}}})

that, as shown in the main body of the text, restricts to a functor

{\mathcal{I}}(G,Q)\longrightarrow{\mathcal{I}}(M,Q\cap M).

Since
{\mathcal{I}}(M,Q\cap M)
is equipped with an action of
\Sph_{M}
, it makes sense to consider its
M
-tempered subcategory: we then define
{\mathcal{W}}(G,P\supseteq Q)
by the fiber product

\textstyle{{\mathcal{W}}(G,P\supseteq Q)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{I}}(G,Q)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{I}}(M,Q\cap M)^{\mathrm{temp}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{I}}(M,Q\cap M).}

Remark 1.4.6
.

When making definitions like the one above, we need to appeal to tempered objects relative to different reductive groups (
G
or
M
). Hence, to avoid confusions we may use the notations ’
G\mathrm{-}\mathrm{temp}
’ or ’
M\mathrm{-}\mathrm{temp}
’. For instance, in the above diagram, we might have used
{\mathcal{I}}(M,Q\cap M)^{M\mathrm{-}\mathrm{temp}}
to signal that tempered objects of
I(M,Q\cap M)
are taken with respect to the
\Sph_{M}
-action on
{\mathcal{I}}(M,Q\cap M)
given by Proposition
1.4.3
.

In Proposition
4.2.16
, we will prove that the
\Sph_{G}
-action on
{\mathcal{I}}(G,Q)
preserves the full subcategory
{\mathcal{W}}(G,P\supseteq Q)
.
Now observe that, for
P=Q
, the DG category
{\mathcal{W}}(G,P\supseteq Q)
coincides with our old
{\mathcal{W}}(G,P)
.
In general, for any inclusion
P\supseteq Q
, we have a diagram

{\mathcal{W}}(G,P)\leftarrow{\mathcal{W}}(G,P\supseteq Q)\rightarrow{\mathcal{W}}(G,Q)

connecting
{\mathcal{W}}(G,P)
with
{\mathcal{W}}(G,Q)
. Explicitly:

•

The arrow
{\mathcal{W}}(G,P\supseteq Q)\rightarrow{\mathcal{W}}(G,Q)
is fully faithful: it is defined by proving that the structure inclusion
{\mathcal{W}}(G,P\supseteq Q)\subseteq{\mathcal{I}}(G,Q)
lands in
{\mathcal{W}}(G,Q)
.

•

The arrow
{\mathcal{W}}(G,P\supseteq Q)\to{\mathcal{W}}(G,P)
is defined by proving that the composition

{\mathcal{W}}(G,P\supseteq Q)\hookrightarrow\DMod(\Bun_{G}^{Q{\mathrm{-gen}}})\xrightarrow{!\mathit{-push}}\DMod(\Bun_{G}^{P{\mathrm{-gen}}})

lands in
{\mathcal{W}}(G,P)\subseteq\DMod(\Bun_{G}^{P{\mathrm{-gen}}})
.

By construction, these functors are all
\Sph_{G}
-linear.

Thus, we see that the formation of
{\mathcal{W}}(G,P\supseteq Q)
is covariant in
P
and contravariant in
Q
. We express this rigorously by constructing a functor

{\mathcal{W}}(G,-\supseteq-):\Tw(\Par)^{\mathrm{op}}\longrightarrow\Sph_{G}\hmod.

This functoriality is not just a technical exercise with abstract nonsense. One of the main inputs necessary for the construction is the following theorem, which is of interest in its own right.

Theorem 1.4.7
.

For any
P\in\Par
, the constant term functor
\CT_{P,*}:\DMod(\Bun_{G})\to\DMod(\Bun_{M})
sends
G
-tempered objects to
M
-tempered objects.

Remark 1.4.8
.

The statement is obvious for
P=B
, but not so for other parabolics. The corresponding statement on the spectral side says that the functor
\CT_{{\check{P}}}^{spec}:\IndCoh_{\check{\mathcal{N}}}({\LS_{\check{G}}})\to\IndCoh_{\check{\mathcal{N}}}({\LS_{\check{M}}})
sends
\QCoh({\LS_{\check{G}}})
to
\QCoh({\LS_{\check{M}}})
. This is easy to prove using the rules of propagation of singular support and the fact that the map
{\LS_{\check{P}}}\to{\LS_{\check{M}}}
is quasi-smooth.

In fact, what we really need is a statement stronger than the above theorem: the fact that the structure functor
{\mathcal{I}}(G,P)\to\DMod(\Bun_{M})
sends
G
-tempered objects to
M
-tempered objects. This is the first part of Theorem
3.0.2
.

Remark 1.4.9
.

Along the same lines, we prove that the ’non-standard’ Eisenstein series functor
\Eis_{P,*}:\DMod(\Bun_{M})\to\DMod(\Bun_{G})
sends
M
-tempered objects to
G
-tempered objects, while the standard Eisenstein series functor
\Eis_{P,!}:\DMod(\Bun_{M})\to\DMod(\Bun_{G})
sends
M
-anti-tempered objects to
G
-anti-tempered objects. See Theorem
3.0.1
.
Contrarily to the statement for
\CT_{P,*}
, the corresponding facts on the spectral side are not obvious (for instance, see
[
Ber21a
, §1.7]
for a special case).

It is easy to connect
\DMod(\Bun_{G})
with
{\mathcal{W}}(G,P\supseteq Q)
: in one direction, we simply have a composition of obvious functors

{\mathcal{W}}(G,P\supseteq Q)\hookrightarrow{\mathcal{I}}(G,Q)\hookrightarrow\DMod(\Bun_{G}^{Q{\mathrm{-gen}}})\xrightarrow{(p_{Q}^{\mathrm{enh}})_{!}}\DMod(\Bun_{G}).

By definition, the composition of the second and third arrow is the
enhanced Eisenstein functor
, see our Section
2.2
.
In the main body of the text, we will show these arrows right adjoints: we obtain a functor

\gamma_{G,P\supseteq Q}:\DMod(\Bun_{G})\longrightarrow{\mathcal{W}}(G,P\supseteq Q).

We can now state the automorphic gluing theorem:

Theorem 1.4.10
.

The above arrows yield a
\Sph_{G}
-linear equivalence

\gamma_{G}:\DMod(\Bun_{G})\xrightarrow{\;\;\simeq\;\;}\lim_{[P\supseteq Q]\in\Tw(\Par)^{\mathrm{op}}}{\mathcal{W}}(G,P\supseteq Q).

Remark 1.4.11
.

We have already noticed that
{\mathcal{W}}(G,P)={\mathcal{W}}(G,P\supseteq P)
for any
P\in\Par
. We regard these DG categories as the main components of the gluing; the intermediate ones (that is, the DG categories
{\mathcal{W}}(G,P\supseteq Q)
with
P\neq Q
) are necessary to assemble the main components together.
If one wants to avoid twisted arrows, one could consider only the main components and obtain a gluing statement that is weaker in two respects: first, these main components assemble only into a lax-limit, not into a limit; second, the natural functor
\DMod(\Bun_{G})\to\mathbin{\mathop{\operatorname{laxlim}}\displaylimits_{P\in\Par}\,}{\mathcal{W}}(G,P)
that one can write down will be fully faithful, but not an equivalence.

1.5.
Proving that
\gamma_{G}
is an equivalence

Let us sketch our method to prove that the above automorphic gluing functor
\gamma_{G}
is an equivalence. More detailed outlines on the partial steps appear at the beginning of sections
5
and
6
.

Since
\gamma_{G}
is by construction
\Sph_{G}
-linear, it induces a functor on the tempered subcategories:

(\gamma_{G})^{\mathrm{temp}}:\DMod(\Bun_{G})^{\mathrm{temp}}\longrightarrow\lim_{[P\supseteq Q]\in\Tw(\Par)^{\mathrm{op}}}{\mathcal{W}}(G,P\supseteq Q)^{\mathrm{temp}}.

We first check that this functor is an equivalence: for this, we observe that the structure inclusion
{\mathcal{W}}(G,P\supseteq Q)^{\mathrm{temp}}\subseteq{\mathcal{I}}(G,Q)^{\mathrm{temp}}
is an equivalence. Since the latter DG category does not depend on
P
, the above limit on the right-hand-side greatly simplifies (twisted arrows disappear) and we are able to conclude.

Next, we look at the subcategories of anti-tempered objects and formulate a different-looking gluing theorem:

Theorem 1.5.1

(Anti-tempered gluing theorem)
.

There is a natural
\Sph_{G}
-linear equivalence

\beta_{G}:\DMod(\Bun_{G})^{\mathrm{atemp}}\longrightarrow\lim_{R\in(\Par^{\prime})^{\mathrm{op}}}{\mathcal{I}}(G,R)^{\mathrm{atemp}}.

Here
\Par^{\prime}
denotes the poset of proper standard parabolics (so, the parabolic
G
is excluded).

This theorem looks more tractable than Theorem
1.4.10
: the limit is not taken with respect to twisted arrows, and the DG categories
{\mathcal{I}}(G,R)
are simpler than their relatives
{\mathcal{W}}(G,R)
. We will use induction on the semisimple rank of
G
to show that Theorem
1.5.1
implies Theorem
1.4.10
.

It remains to prove Theorem
1.5.1
. By contruction,
\beta_{G}
is equipped with a left adjoint
\beta_{G}^{L}
, so we need to show that the unit and counit of the adjunction are both isomorphisms.

The proof for the counit
\beta_{G}^{L}\circ\beta_{G}\to id
follows by combining the Deligne-Lusztig duality of the second author (see
[
Che20a
]
) with the Ramanujan conjecture of the first author (see
[
Ber21b
]
).

Finally, the proof for the unit
id\to\beta_{G}\circ\beta_{G}^{L}
is one of the core results of this paper and it brings together several techniques: Weyl combinatorics, miraculous duality, Drinfeld compactifications, etc. We refer to the beginning of Section
6
for a detailed outline of the strategy.

1.6.
Structure of the paper

In Section
2
, we recall some essential preliminary notions: tempered and anti-tempered objects, Eisenstein series and constant term functors, the prestack
\Bun_{G}^{P{\mathrm{-gen}}}
and the parabolic DG category
{\mathcal{I}}(G,P)
. Then we show that
\Sph_{G}
naturally acts on
{\mathcal{I}}(G,P)
; as explained in the discussions above, this action is necessary for our constructions and proofs.

In Section
3
, we prove another important result, Theorem
1.4.7
, on preservation of tempered objects. Actually, we prove more results of similar kind, see Theorem
3.0.1
and Theorem
3.0.2
for the full list. These statements seem to be of interest in their own right. They are proven by passing to a local situation: we consider the
semi-infinite
parabolic category
\SI_{P}
and analyze its relation with
\Sph_{M}
and
\Sph_{G}
.

In Section
4
, we use the results of the previous sections to construct the DG categories
{\mathcal{W}}(G,P\supseteq Q)
and study their functoriality via twisted arrows. We can then define the automorphic gluing functor

\gamma_{G}:\DMod(\Bun_{G})\to\lim_{[P\supseteq Q]\in\Tw(\Par)^{\mathrm{op}}}{\mathcal{W}}(G,P\supseteq Q)

and its left adjoint.

In Section
5
, we first state the anti-tempered gluing theorem, that is, the fact that a natural functor

\beta_{G}:\DMod(\Bun_{G})^{\mathrm{atemp}}\to\lim_{R\in\Par^{\prime}}{\mathcal{I}}(G,R)^{\mathrm{atemp}}

is an equivalence. We use induction on the semisimple rank of
G
to prove that this theorem implies our main theorem.

Finally, in Section
6
, we prove the anti-tempered gluing theorem, that is, the fact that
\beta_{G}
is an equivalence. As mentioned, the most difficult part is to prove that
\beta_{G}
is essentially surjective.

1.7.
Acknowledgements

We are grateful to Dennis Gaitsgory for teaching us most of the mathematics used in this paper. The first author thanks Dima Arinkin, Ian Grojnowski and Sam Raskin for several conversations and suggestions. The second author thanks Kevin Lin for a discussion on Weyl combinatorics.
The work of the second author was supported by a grant from the Simons Foundation (816048, LC) during his stay at the Institute for Advanced Study.

2.
Preliminaries

In this section, we recall some of the main players of this paper. We fix a parabolic subgroup
P\subseteq G
throughout. Let
U
be its unipotent radical and
M=P/U
be its Levi quotient group.

In §
2.1
, we recall the notion of
G
-tempered objects
in a category acted on by
\Sph_{G}
.
In §
2.2
, we recall the
(geometric) Eisenstein series functors

\Eis_{P,!},\Eis_{P,*}
and the
constant term functors

\CT_{P,!},\CT_{P,*}
.
In §
2.3
, we recall the
parabolic category

{\mathcal{I}}(G,P)
and its properties.
Finally, §
2.4
is dedicated to the proof of Proposition
2.3.15
: this results equips
{\mathcal{I}}(G,P)
with a natural action of
\Sph_{G}
, thereby letting us consider
G
-tempered objects in
{\mathcal{I}}(G,P)
.

2.1.
G
-temperedness

In this subsection, we recall the notion of temperedness. Details and proofs can be found in
[
Ber21b
]
and
[
Ber21c
]
.

Consider the monoidal category

\Sph_{G}:=\DMod(G({\mathcal{O}})\backslash G({\mathcal{K}})/G({\mathcal{O}}))

with monoidal structure given by convolution. Recall the
derived Satake equivalence
(see
[
AG15
, §12]
):

\Sat_{G}:\Sph_{G}\simeq\IndCoh_{\Nilp(\check{\mathfrak{g}}^{*})/\check{G}}(\pt\mathbin{\mathop{\times}\displaylimits_{\check{\mathfrak{g}}}}\pt/\check{G}),

where the RHS is a certain full subcategory of
\IndCoh(\pt\mathbin{\mathop{\times}\displaylimits_{\check{\mathfrak{g}}}}\pt/\check{G})
that contains
\QCoh(\pt\mathbin{\mathop{\times}\displaylimits_{\check{\mathfrak{g}}}}\pt/\check{G})
.

Definition 2.1.1
.

Define

\Sph_{G}^{\mathrm{temp}}:=\Sat_{G}^{-1}(\QCoh(\pt\mathbin{\mathop{\times}\displaylimits_{\check{\mathfrak{g}}}}\pt/\check{G}))\subseteq\Sph_{G},

which is a two-sided monoidal ideal of
\Sph_{G}
. Objects in this full subcategory are called
G
-tempered
, or simply
tempered
.

The category
\Sph_{G}^{\mathrm{temp}}
is compactly generated and the inclusion functor
\Sph_{G}^{\mathrm{temp}}\to\Sph_{G}
preserves compact objects. It has a continuous right adjoint

\mathrm{temp}_{G}:\Sph_{G}\to\Sph_{G}^{\mathrm{temp}}

called the
(
G
-)temperization functor
.

Definition 2.1.2
.

Define

\Sph_{G}^{\mathrm{atemp}}:=\ker(\mathrm{temp}_{G})\subseteq\Sph_{G}

to be the kernel of
\mathrm{temp}_{G}
, which is also a two-sided monoidal ideal of
\Sph_{G}
. Objects in this full subcategory are called
(
G
-)anti-tempered
.

The category
\Sph_{G}^{\mathrm{atemp}}
is also compactly generated, and the inclusion functor
\Sph_{G}^{\mathrm{atemp}}\to\Sph_{G}
has a left adjoint

\mathrm{atemp}_{G}:\Sph_{G}\to\Sph_{G}^{\mathrm{atemp}}.

Then we also have

\Sph_{G}^{\mathrm{temp}}\simeq\ker(\mathrm{atemp}_{G})\subseteq\Sph_{G}

Consider the perverse t-structure on
\Sph_{G}
, i.e., that induced from the perverse t-structure on
\DMod(\Gr_{G})
. We have

Lemma 2.1.3

(​​
[
Ber21b
, Corollary 2.3.7]
)
.

The full subcategory
\Sph_{G}^{\mathrm{atemp}}\subseteq\Sph_{G}
is equivalent to
\Sph_{G}^{\leq-\infty}
.

Definition 2.1.4
.

For any left
\Sph_{G}
-module category
{\mathcal{C}}
, define

{\mathcal{C}}^{G\mathrm{-}\mathrm{temp}}:=\Sph_{G}^{\mathrm{temp}}\mathbin{\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits_{\Sph_{G}}}{\mathcal{C}},\;{\mathcal{C}}^{G\mathrm{-}\mathrm{atemp}}:=\Sph_{G}^{\mathrm{atemp}}\mathbin{\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits_{\Sph_{G}}}{\mathcal{C}}.

The
\Sph_{G}
-linear adjoint functors

\Sph_{G}^{\mathrm{temp}}\adjoint\Sph_{G}\adjoint\Sph_{G}^{\mathrm{atemp}}

induce adjoint functors

{\mathcal{C}}^{G\mathrm{-}\mathrm{temp}}\adjoint{\mathcal{C}}\adjoint{\mathcal{C}}^{G\mathrm{-}\mathrm{atemp}}.

We view
{\mathcal{C}}^{G\mathrm{-}\mathrm{temp}}
and
{\mathcal{C}}^{G\mathrm{-}\mathrm{atemp}}
as full subcategories of
{\mathcal{C}}
: these are the full subcategories of
(
G
-)tempered
and
(
G
-)anti-tempered
objects of
{\mathcal{C}}
. We still have

{\mathcal{C}}^{G\mathrm{-atemp}}\simeq\ker(\mathrm{temp}_{G})\subseteq{\mathcal{C}},\;\;{\mathcal{C}}^{G\mathrm{-temp}}\simeq\ker(\mathrm{atemp}_{G})\subseteq{\mathcal{C}}

We define similar notions for right
\Sph_{G}
-module categories. Equivalently, they can be defined by viewing any right
\Sph_{G}
-module category as a left one via the anti-monoidal involution on
\Sph_{G}
induced by taking the inverse in
G({\mathcal{O}})\backslash G({\mathcal{K}})/G({\mathcal{O}})
.

Definition 2.1.5
.

Define

\mathbf{1}_{\Sph_{G}}^{\mathrm{temp}}:=\mathrm{temp}_{G}(\mathbf{1}_{\Sph_{G}})

to be the temperization of the monoidal unit of
\Sph_{G}
. We call this object the
tempered monoidal unit
of
\Sph_{G}
.

Lemma 2.1.6
.

For any left
\Sph_{G}
-module category
{\mathcal{C}}
and object
{\mathcal{F}}\in{\mathcal{C}}
, we have

•

{\mathcal{F}}
is tempered iff there is an isomorphism
\mathbf{1}_{\Sph_{G}}^{\mathrm{temp}}\mathbin{\mathop{\star}\displaylimits}{\mathcal{F}}\simeq{\mathcal{F}}
;

•

{\mathcal{F}}
is anti-tempered iff
\mathbf{1}_{\Sph_{G}}^{\mathrm{temp}}\mathbin{\mathop{\star}\displaylimits}{\mathcal{F}}\simeq 0
.

Proof.

Follows from the definitions.

∎[Lemma
2.1.6
]

Example 2.1.7
.

When
G=T
is a torus, we have
\Sph_{T}^{\mathrm{temp}}=\Sph_{T}
. Hence
{\mathcal{C}}^{T\mathrm{-}\mathrm{temp}}={\mathcal{C}}
.

Example 2.1.8
.

For any fixed closed point
x\in X
and fixed identification
{\mathcal{O}}_{x}\simeq{\mathcal{O}}
, consider the
\Sph_{G}
-action on
\DMod(\Bun_{G})
given by Hecke modifications at
{\mathcal{O}}_{x}\simeq{\mathcal{O}}
. By
[
FR21
]
, the resulting subcategory
\DMod(\Bun_{G})^{G\mathrm{-}\mathrm{temp}}
does
not
depend on the choice of
x
and
{\mathcal{O}}_{x}\simeq{\mathcal{O}}
.

We also need the following technical lemma:

Lemma 2.1.9
.

For any left
\Sph_{G}
-module category
{\mathcal{C}}
, if
{\mathcal{C}}
is compactly generated, then so is
{\mathcal{C}}^{G\mathrm{-}\mathrm{temp}}
, and the functor
{\mathcal{C}}^{G\mathrm{-}\mathrm{temp}}\to{\mathcal{C}}
preserves compact objects.

Proof.

Note that the second claim follows from the first one because the functor
{\mathcal{C}}^{G\mathrm{-}\mathrm{temp}}\to{\mathcal{C}}
has a continuous right adjoint
\mathrm{temp}_{G}
.

By
[
DG15
, Corollary 1.9.4]
, we only need to show the acting functors
\Sph_{G}\mathbin{\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits}{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{C}}
and
\Sph_{G}^{\mathrm{temp}}\mathbin{\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits}\Sph_{G}\to\Sph_{G}^{\mathrm{temp}}
have continuous right adjoints. This follows from Lemma
A.1.6
and Lemma
A.1.7
.

∎[Lemma
2.1.9
]

2.2.
The Eisenstein series and the constant term functors

In this subsection, we recall the Eisenstein series and the constant term functors. Details and proof can be found in
[
BG02
]
,
[
DG16
]
.

Consider the following diagram

\Bun_{M}\xleftarrow{q}\Bun_{P}\xrightarrow{p}\Bun_{G}.

The map
q
is quasi-compact, smooth and universally homologically contractible. The latter property means that, for any base-change
q^{\prime}
of
q
, the functor
(q^{\prime})^{!}
is fully faithful. The map
p
is schematic and quasi-compact when restricted to each connected component of the source.

Definition 2.2.1
.

Define the following four functors:

\displaystyle\Eis_{P,!}=p_{!}\circ q^{*},\,\Eis_{P,*}=p_{*}\circ q^{!}:\DMod(\Bun_{M})\to\DMod(\Bun_{G});

\displaystyle\CT_{P,!}=q_{!}\circ p^{*},\,\CT_{P,*}=q_{*}\circ p^{!}:\DMod(\Bun_{G})\to\DMod(\Bun_{M}).

Remark 2.2.2
.

Even though
p_{!},p^{*}
and
q_{!},q^{*}
are only partially defined functors, the compositions
p_{!}\circ q^{*}
and
q_{!}\circ p^{*}
are well-defined.

Theorem 2.2.3

(2nd adjointness for
\Bun_{G}
,
[
DG16
, Theorem 1.2.3]
)
.

For a pair of opposite parabolic subgroups
(P,P^{-})
of
G
, there is a canonical equivalence
\CT_{P^{-},!}\simeq\CT_{P,*}
.

2.3.
The parabolic category
{\mathcal{I}}(G,P)

In this subsection, we review the definition of the parabolic category
{\mathcal{I}}(G,P)
.

Consider the prestack
\Bun_{G}^{P{\mathrm{-gen}}}:=\mathbf{Maps}_{\mathrm{gen}}(X,{\mathbb{B}}G\leftarrow{\mathbb{B}}P)
classifying
G
-torsors on
X
equipped with generic reductions to
P
(see
[
Bar12
]
, and also
[
Che20a
, §0.2]
). We have a diagram

\Bun_{M}\xleftarrow{q}\Bun_{P}\xrightarrow{\iota_{P}}\Bun_{G}^{P{\mathrm{-gen}}}.

Remark 2.3.1
.

The prestack
\Bun_{G}^{P{\mathrm{-gen}}}
is
not
an algebraic stack because it does not have an atlas. Nevertheless, it is a reasonable geometric object. Namely, by
[
Bar12
, Remark 4.1.9]
,
\Bun_{G}^{P{\mathrm{-gen}}}
is isomorphic to the fppf sheafification of a simplicial colimit
\mathbin{\mathop{\operatorname{colim}}\displaylimits_{\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}}}\,}Y_{\bullet}
such that
Y_{n}
are algebraic stacks and each connected component of
Y_{n}
is proper over
\Bun_{G}
. In fact, one can choose
Y_{0}:=\widetilde{\Bun}_{P}
or
\overline{\Bun}_{P}
to be the Drinfeld compactifications (see
[
BG02
]
) and
Y_{\bullet}
be the Cech nerve of the map
Y_{0}\to\Bun_{G}^{P{\mathrm{-gen}}}
. It follows that we have

(10)

\DMod(\Bun_{G}^{P{\mathrm{-gen}}})\simeq\lim_{[n]\in\mathbf{\Delta},!\mathrm{-pull}}\DMod(Y_{n})\simeq\mathbin{\mathop{\operatorname{colim}}\displaylimits_{[n]\in\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}},!\mathrm{-push}}\,}\DMod(Y_{n}).

The following result is implicit in
[
Che20a
, Appendix B]
:

Lemma 2.3.2
.

For any affine finite type test scheme
S\to\Bun_{G}^{P{\mathrm{-gen}}}
, the base-change
\Bun_{P}\mathbin{\mathop{\times}\displaylimits_{\Bun_{G}^{P{\mathrm{-gen}}}}}S
is a finite disjoint union of a locally closed subschemes of
S
, and the map
\Bun_{P}\mathbin{\mathop{\times}\displaylimits_{\Bun_{G}^{P{\mathrm{-gen}}}}}S\to S
is both injective and surjective. In particular,
\Bun_{P}\to\Bun_{G}^{P{\mathrm{-gen}}}
is qcqs schematic.

Proof.

By Remark
2.3.1
and fppf descent, we can assume that
S\to\Bun_{G}^{P{\mathrm{-gen}}}
factors through
\widetilde{\Bun}_{P}
. By
[
Che20a
, §B.1.3]
, there is a Cartesian square

(11)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}_{\mathrm{dfstr}}\widetilde{\Bun}_{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Bun_{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\Bun}_{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Bun_{G}^{P{\mathrm{-gen}}},}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\iota}_{P}}

where
{}_{\mathrm{dfstr}}\widetilde{\Bun}_{P}
is the disjoint union of the defect strata of
\widetilde{\Bun}_{P}
. This implies the claim of the lemma.

∎[Lemma
2.3.2
]

The above lemma suggests
\Bun_{G}^{P{\mathrm{-gen}}}
should be viewed as glued from the connected components of
\Bun_{P}
. Hence
\DMod(\Bun_{G}^{P{\mathrm{-gen}}})
should be viewed as glued from the direct summands of
\DMod(\Bun_{P})
. Recall the functor
q^{*}:\DMod(\Bun_{M})\to\DMod(\Bun_{P})
is fully faithful. Hence the above gluing induces a full subcategory of
\DMod(\Bun_{G}^{P{\mathrm{-gen}}})
glued from the direct summands of
\DMod(\Bun_{M})
. More precisely, we have the following definition:

Definition 2.3.3

(​​
[
Che20a
, Definition 0.2.4]
)
.

Define
{\mathcal{I}}(G,P)\subseteq\DMod(\Bun_{G}^{P{\mathrm{-gen}}})
to be the full subcategory such that we have the following Cartesian diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{I}}(G,P)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\DMod(\Bun_{G}^{P{\mathrm{-gen}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\DMod(\Bun_{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\DMod(\Bun_{P}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subseteq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{M}^{!}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{P}^{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subseteq}

Remark 2.3.4
.

Since the map
q
is smooth, in the above definition, we can use
q^{!}
instead of
q^{*}
and obtain the same full subcategory
{\mathcal{I}}(G,P)
.

Warning 2.3.5
.

The notation
\iota_{M}^{!}
is only heuristic: it is
not
induced by
!
-pullback along any (well-behaved) map
\iota_{M}
.

In fact,
{\mathcal{I}}(G,P)
is equivalent to the category of D-modules on the pushout
\Bun_{M}\sqcup_{\Bun_{P}}\Bun_{G}^{P{\mathrm{-gen}}}
. However, this prestack is ill-behaved. Then
\iota_{M}
can be viewed as the map

\Bun_{M}\to\Bun_{M}\sqcup_{\Bun_{P}}\Bun_{G}^{P{\mathrm{-gen}}}.

Remark 2.3.6
.

Let us also provide the analogue picture in number theory. The stack
\Bun_{M}
(resp.
\Bun_{P}
,
\Bun_{G}^{P{\mathrm{-gen}}}
) is an algebro-geoemtric incarnation of the double quotient
M(F)\backslash M({\mathbb{A}})/M({\mathbb{O}})
(resp.
P(F)\backslash P({\mathbb{A}})/P({\mathbb{O}})
,
P(F)\backslash G({\mathbb{A}})/G({\mathbb{O}})
), where
F
is the field of rational functions on
X
and
{\mathbb{A}}
(resp.
{\mathbb{O}}
) is the ring of adeles (resp. integral adeles) of
F
. Therefore the above prestack should be viewed as an algebro-geometric incarnation of
U({\mathbb{A}})M(F)\backslash G({\mathbb{A}})/G({\mathbb{O}})
, which is indeed isomorphic to
M(F)\backslash M({\mathbb{A}})/M({\mathbb{O}})
as sets.

Lemma 2.3.7

(​​
[
Che20a
, Corollary 0.2.8]
))
.

We have adjoint functors:

\iota_{M,!}:\DMod(\Bun_{M})\adjoint{\mathcal{I}}(G,P):\iota_{M}^{!},

where
\iota_{M}^{!}
is induced by
\iota_{P}^{!}
and is conservative, while
\iota_{M,!}
is induced by
\iota_{P,!}\circ q^{*}
. The category
{\mathcal{I}}(G,P)
is compactly generated by the image of compact objects of
\DMod(\Bun_{M})
under the functor
\iota_{M,!}
.

Definition 2.3.8
.

Write
\oblv^{U({\mathbb{A}})}:{\mathcal{I}}(G,P)\to\DMod(\Bun_{G}^{P{\mathrm{-gen}}})
for the forgetful functor. The above lemma implies it preserves compact objects, hence it admits a continuous right adjiont

\Av_{*}^{U({\mathbb{A}})}:\DMod(\Bun_{G}^{P{\mathrm{-gen}}})\to{\mathcal{I}}(G,P).

In above functor, the notation
U({\mathbb{A}})
is motivated by Remark
2.3.6
.

Definition 2.3.9
.

Recall that the connected components
\Bun_{M,\lambda}
of
\Bun_{M}
(resp.
\Bun_{P}
) are labelled by
\lambda\in\Lambda_{G,P}
, which is the quotient of the coweight lattice
\Lambda_{G}
by the
{\mathbb{Z}}
-span of simple coroots contained in
M
. Hence we obtain a
\Lambda_{G,P}
-grading on
\DMod(\Bun_{M})
. Let
\Bun_{P,\lambda}
and
\widetilde{\Bun}_{P,\lambda}
be the inverse images of
\Bun_{M,\lambda}
along the maps
\Bun_{P}\to\widetilde{\Bun}_{P}\to\Bun_{M}
.

For each
\lambda\in\Lambda_{G,P}
, we have adjoint functors

(\iota_{M,\lambda})_{!}:\DMod(\Bun_{M,\lambda})\adjoint{\mathcal{I}}(G,P):\iota_{M,\lambda}^{!}.

The partial order
\geq
on
\Lambda_{G}
induces a partial order
\geq
on
\Lambda_{G,P}
. Let
\DMod(\Bun_{M})^{\geq\lambda}:=\bigoplus_{\mu\geq\lambda}\DMod(\Bun_{M,\mu})
and let

{\mathcal{I}}(G,P)^{\geq\lambda}\subseteq{\mathcal{I}}(G,P)
be the full subcategory generated under colimits by the image of
\iota_{M,!}(\DMod(\Bun_{M})^{\geq\lambda})
. Equivalently, it is the full subcategory of objects
{\mathcal{F}}
such that
\iota_{M,\mu}^{!}({\mathcal{F}})\simeq 0
unless
\mu\geq\lambda
.

We obtain a (decreasing) filtration on
{\mathcal{I}}(G,P)
indexed by the poset
(\Lambda_{G,P},\geq)
.

Remark 2.3.10
.

The category
{\mathcal{I}}(G,P)^{\geq\lambda}\subseteq{\mathcal{I}}(G,P)
should be viewed as the category of D-modules supported on a closed sub-prestack of
\Bun_{M}\sqcup_{\Bun_{P}}\Bun_{G}^{P{\mathrm{-gen}}}
.

Lemma 2.3.11
.

The functor
\iota_{M,!}
and
\iota_{M}^{!}
are compatible with the above filtrations on
\DMod(\Bun_{M})
and
{\mathcal{I}}(G,P)
. Also, the endo-functor
\mathrm{coFib}(\Id\to\iota_{M}^{!}\circ\iota_{M,!})
on
\DMod(\Bun_{M})
strictly increases the grading.

Proof.

The functor
\iota_{M,!}
is obviously compatible with the filtrations. To prove this for
\iota_{M}^{!}
, we only need to show that the endo-functor
\iota_{M}^{!}\circ\iota_{M,!}
preserves
\DMod(\Bun_{M})^{\geq\lambda}
. In other words, we need to show
\iota_{M,\mu}^{!}\circ(\iota_{M,\lambda})_{!}\simeq 0
unless
\mu\geq\lambda
. By definition, we only need to prove the corresponding claim for
\iota_{P,\mu}^{!}\circ(\iota_{P,\lambda})_{!}
, where
\iota_{P,\lambda}
is the map
\Bun_{P,\lambda}\to\Bun_{G}^{P{\mathrm{-gen}}}
. Consider the diagram (
11
). It suffices to prove that
\iota_{P,\mu}^{!}\circ(\widetilde{\iota}_{P,\lambda})_{!}\simeq 0
unless
\mu\geq\lambda
.
By proper base-change, it suffices to show that the fiber product

\widetilde{\Bun}_{P,\lambda}\mathbin{\mathop{\times}\displaylimits_{\Bun_{G}^{P{\mathrm{-gen}}}}}\Bun_{P,\mu}

is empty unless
\mu\geq\lambda
. In other words, we need to show the following: if
\widetilde{\Bun}_{P,\lambda}
has a defect stratum over
\Bun_{P,\mu}
, then
\mu\geq\lambda
. But this was proved in
[
BG02
]
. This concludes the proof that
\iota_{M}^{!}
is compatible with the filtrations.

For the second claim, we just need to show that
\Id\to\iota_{M,\lambda}^{!}\circ(\iota_{M,\lambda})_{!}
is invertible. This follows from diagram (
11
) and the fact that
\Bun_{P,\lambda}
is an open stratum of
\widetilde{\Bun}_{P,\lambda}
.

∎[Lemma
2.3.11
]

Corollary 2.3.12
.

The functor
\iota_{M}^{!}
has a unique
continuous
right inverse
\iota_{M,*}
, and the functor
\iota_{M,!}
has a unique
continuous
left inverse
\iota_{M}^{*}
. These functors are compatible with the filtrations on
\DMod(\Bun_{M})
and
{\mathcal{I}}(G,P)
. Also, we have adjoint functors:

\iota_{M}^{*}:{\mathcal{I}}(G,P)\adjoint\DMod(\Bun_{M,\lambda}):(\iota_{M})_{*}.

Proof.

The proof below is standard, i.e., essentially the same as that for similar statements about D-modules on stratified spaces.

Consider the functor
\iota_{P,*}
. Note that
\Bun_{P}\mathbin{\mathop{\times}\displaylimits_{\Bun_{G}^{P{\mathrm{-gen}}}}}\Bun_{P}\simeq\Bun_{P}
. Hence we have
\iota_{P}^{!}\circ\iota_{P,*}\simeq\Id
. It follows that
\iota_{P,*}
sends the image of
q^{*}
into
{\mathcal{I}}(G,P)
. Let
\iota_{M,*}:\DMod(\Bun_{M})\to{\mathcal{I}}(G,P)
to the composition
\iota_{P,*}\circ q^{*}
. By construction, it is a right inverse of
\iota_{M}^{!}
. The left adjoint
\iota_{M,\lambda}^{*}
of each
(\iota_{M,\lambda})_{*}
is well-defined because the composition
\iota_{M,\lambda}^{*}\circ\iota_{M,!}
, which is the restriction functor
\DMod(\Bun_{M})\to\DMod(\Bun_{M,\lambda})
, is well-defined.

By construction, the functor
\iota_{M,*}
and
\iota_{M}^{*}:=\oplus_{\lambda}\iota_{M,\lambda}^{*}
are compatible with filtrations.

To prove the adjoint pair
(\iota_{M}^{*},\iota_{M,*})
, we only need to show
\bigoplus(\iota_{M,\lambda})_{*}\to\prod(\iota_{M,\lambda})_{*}
is an equivalence. The conservative functor
\iota_{M}^{!}
obviously commutes with colimits, and commutes with limits because it has a left adjoint
\iota_{M,!}
. Hence we only need to show
\bigoplus\iota_{M}^{!}\circ(\iota_{M,\lambda})_{*}\to\prod\iota_{M}^{!}\circ(\iota_{M,\lambda})_{*}
is an equivalence but this is obvious.

It remains to prove the uniqueness. By the Barr–Beck–Lurie theorem,
{\mathcal{I}}(G,P)\simeq(\iota_{M}^{!}\circ\iota_{M,!})\mathrm{-mod}(\DMod(\Bun_{M}))
. By Lemma
2.3.11
, for each object in
\DMod(\Bun_{M,\lambda})\subseteq\DMod(\Bun_{M})
, there is a unique
(\iota_{M}^{!}\circ\iota_{M,!})
-module structure on it. Unwinding the definitions, this means there is a unique functor
F_{\lambda}:\DMod(\Bun_{M,\lambda})\to{\mathcal{I}}(G,P)
such that
\iota_{M,\mu}^{!}\circ F_{\lambda}\simeq 0
for
\mu\neq\lambda
and
\iota_{M,\lambda}^{!}\circ F_{\lambda}\simeq\Id
. Therefore any
continuous
right inverse of
\iota_{M}^{!}
must be
\oplus_{\lambda}F_{\lambda}
. This proves the uniqueness of
F
.

On the other hand, for any continuous left inverse
E
of
\iota_{M,!}
, its restriction
E_{\lambda}:{\mathcal{I}}(G,P)\to\DMod(\Bun_{M,\lambda})
is continuous and satisfies
E_{\lambda}\circ(\iota_{M,\mu})_{!}\simeq 0
for
\mu\neq\lambda
and
E_{\lambda}\circ(\iota_{M,\lambda})_{!}\simeq\Id
. Passing to right adjoints, we see
F_{\lambda}
is the right adjoint of
E_{\lambda}
. Hence
E
is also unique.

∎[Corollary
2.3.12
]

Definition 2.3.13
.

Let
{\mathcal{I}}(G,P)^{\iota_{*}{\mathrm{-gen}}}\subseteq{\mathcal{I}}(G,P)
be the full subcategory generated under colimits by the image of
\iota_{M,*}:\DMod(\Bun_{M})\to{\mathcal{I}}(G,P)
.

Corollary 2.3.14
.

For
\lambda\in\Lambda_{G,P}
, suppose
{\mathcal{F}}\in{\mathcal{I}}(G,P)
is such that
\iota_{M,\mu}^{!}({\mathcal{F}})\simeq 0
unless
\mu\leq\lambda
, then
{\mathcal{F}}\in{\mathcal{I}}(G,P)^{\iota_{*}{\mathrm{-gen}}}
.

Proof.

Follows from Lemma
2.3.11
and Corollary
2.3.12
by a standard argument.

∎[Corollary
2.3.14
]

The following result seems to be well-known but we can not find a proof in the literature. We provide a proof in §
2.4
.

Proposition 2.3.15
.

For any parabolic subgroup
P
of
G
and fixed closed point
x\in X
, consider the
\Sph_{G,x}
-action on
\DMod(\Bun_{G}^{P{\mathrm{-gen}}})
given by Hecke modifications at
x
. Then this action preserves
{\mathcal{I}}(G,P)\subseteq\DMod(\Bun_{G}^{P{\mathrm{-gen}}})
.

Now we study the functorial properties of the parabolic categories
{\mathcal{I}}(G,P)
.

Definition 2.3.16
.

For any parabolic subgroups
Q\subseteq P
of
G
, define adjoint functors

\Eis_{Q\to P}^{\mathrm{enh}}:{\mathcal{I}}(G,Q)\adjoint{\mathcal{I}}(G,P):\CT_{P\leftarrow Q}^{\mathrm{enh}}

as follows. Consider the map

p_{Q\to P}^{\mathrm{enh}}:\Bun_{G}^{Q{\mathrm{-gen}}}\to\Bun_{G}^{P{\mathrm{-gen}}}

sending a generic
Q
-reduction to its induced
P
-reduction. The functor
(p_{Q\to P}^{\mathrm{enh}})_{!}
is well defined (see Remark
2.3.18
below) and sends
{\mathcal{I}}(G,Q)
into
{\mathcal{I}}(G,P)
(see
[
Che20a
, Proposition 0.2.10]
). The left adjoint
\Eis_{Q\to P}^{\mathrm{enh}}
is defined as its restriction.

The functor
\CT_{P\leftarrow Q}^{\mathrm{enh}}
is defined as its continuous right adjoint. By definition, we have

\CT_{P\leftarrow Q}^{\mathrm{enh}}\simeq\Av_{*}^{U_{Q}({\mathbb{A}})}\circ(p_{Q\to P}^{\mathrm{enh}})^{!}.

Warning 2.3.17
.

The functor
(p_{Q\to P}^{\mathrm{enh}})^{!}
does not sends
{\mathcal{I}}(G,P)
to
{\mathcal{I}}(G,Q)
.

Remark 2.3.18
.

Recall we have a map
\overline{\Bun}_{Q}\to\overline{\Bun}_{P}
compatible with the map
p_{Q\to P}^{\mathrm{enh}}:\Bun_{G}^{Q{\mathrm{-gen}}}\to\Bun_{G}^{P{\mathrm{-gen}}}
. Hence by the equivalence (
10
) (with the choice
Y_{0}:=\overline{\Bun}_{P}
), well-definedness of
(p_{Q\to P}^{\mathrm{enh}})_{!}
follows from well-definedness of proper
!
-pushforward functors.

Remark 2.3.19
.

By definition, we have

\Eis_{P,!}\simeq\Eis_{P\to G}^{\mathrm{enh}}\circ\iota_{M,!},\;\;\CT_{P,*}\simeq\iota_{M}^{!}\circ\CT_{G\leftarrow P}^{\mathrm{enh}}.

On the other hand, by Remark
2.3.1
, the functor
(p^{\mathrm{enh}}_{P\to G})_{!}
can be calculated as the simplicial colimit of

\DMod(\Bun_{G}^{P{\mathrm{-gen}}})\xrightarrow{!\mathrm{-pull}}\DMod(Y_{n})\xrightarrow{!\mathrm{-push}}\DMod(\Bun_{G}),

where
Y_{n}
is the Cech nerve of the map
\widetilde{\Bun}_{P}\to\Bun_{G}
. Note that the above
!
-pushforward is also
*
-pushforward. It follows that the composition

\DMod(\Bun_{P})\xrightarrow{*\mathrm{-push}}\DMod(\Bun_{G}^{P{\mathrm{-gen}}})\xrightarrow{!\mathrm{-push}}\DMod(\Bun_{G})

is equivalent to the
*
-pushforward functor. In particular,

\Eis_{P,*}\simeq\Eis_{P\to G}^{\mathrm{enh}}\circ\iota_{M,*}\circ[2\mathrm{rel.dim.}(\Bun_{P},\Bun_{M})],

where
\mathrm{rel.dim.}(\Bun_{P},\Bun_{M})
is the locally constant function on
\Bun_{M}
whose values are the dimensions of the fibers of
\Bun_{P}\to\Bun_{M}
.

The following result will be used repeatedly in this paper without explicit reference:

Lemma 2.3.20
.

For any inclusion
Q\subseteq P
of parabolic subgroups of
G
, consider the
\Sph_{G,x}
-actions on
{\mathcal{I}}(G,Q)
and
{\mathcal{I}}(G,P)
. The adjoint functors

\Eis:{\mathcal{I}}(G,Q)\adjoint{\mathcal{I}}(G,P):\CT

are canonically
\Sph_{G,x}
-linear.

Proof.

The
!
-pullback functor along
\Bun_{G}^{Q{\mathrm{-gen}}}\to\Bun_{G}^{P{\mathrm{-gen}}}
is canonically
\Sph_{G,x}
-linear by the base-change isomorphisms. By definition,
\CT
is the composition of this functor with the localization functor
\DMod(\Bun_{G}^{Q{\mathrm{-gen}}})\to{\mathcal{I}}(G,Q)
, i.e., with the right adjoint to the embedding
{\mathcal{I}}(G,Q)\to\DMod(\Bun_{G}^{Q{\mathrm{-gen}}})
. By definition, this embedding is canonically
\Sph_{G,x}
-linear, hence so is its right adjoint (Appendix
A
). It follows that
\CT
is canonically
\Sph_{G,x}
-linear. Using Appendix
A
again, its left adjoint
\Eis
is also canonically
\Sph_{G,x}
-linear.

∎[Lemma
2.3.20
]

2.4.
Proof of Proposition
2.3.15

In this subsection, we prove Proposition
2.3.15
.

For
{\mathcal{M}}\in{\mathcal{I}}(G,P)
and
{\mathcal{F}}\in\Sph_{G,x}
, we need to show that
{\mathcal{F}}\mathbin{\mathop{\star}\displaylimits_{\Sph_{G,x}}}{\mathcal{M}}\in{\mathcal{I}}(G,P)
. Consider the prestack
\Hecke_{G,x}^{P{\mathrm{-gen}}}
classifying quadruples
({\mathcal{P}}_{G}^{l},{\mathcal{P}}_{G}^{r},\theta,{\mathcal{P}}_{P}^{\mathrm{gen}})
, where:

•

{\mathcal{P}}_{G}^{l}
and
{\mathcal{P}}_{G}^{r}
are
G
-torsors on
X
;

•

\theta:{\mathcal{P}}_{G}^{l}|_{X\setminus x}\to{\mathcal{P}}_{G}^{r}|_{X\setminus x}
is an isomorphism, which in particular allows to identify the generic
G
-torsors underlying
{\mathcal{P}}_{G}^{l}
and
{\mathcal{P}}_{G}^{r}
;

•

{\mathcal{P}}_{P}^{\mathrm{gen}}
is a
P
-reduction of this generic
G
-torsor.

Let
h_{l},h_{r}:\Gr_{G,x}\widetilde{\mathbin{\mathop{\times}\displaylimits}}\Bun_{G}^{P{\mathrm{-gen}}}\to\Bun_{G}^{P{\mathrm{-gen}}}
be the maps sending the above data to
({\mathcal{P}}_{G}^{l},{\mathcal{P}}_{P}^{\mathrm{gen}})
and
({\mathcal{P}}_{G}^{r},{\mathcal{P}}_{P}^{\mathrm{gen}})
respectively. Note that the map
h_{r}
witnesses
\Hecke_{G,x}^{P{\mathrm{-gen}}}
as a twisted product

\Hecke_{G,x}^{P{\mathrm{-gen}}}\simeq\Gr_{G,x}\widetilde{\mathbin{\mathop{\times}\displaylimits}}\Bun_{G}^{P{\mathrm{-gen}}}.

Then we have

{\mathcal{F}}\mathbin{\mathop{\star}\displaylimits_{\Sph_{G,x}}}{\mathcal{M}}\simeq h_{l,*}({\mathcal{F}}\widetilde{\boxtimes}{\mathcal{M}}).

Write
\iota_{P}:\Bun_{P}\to\Bun_{G}^{P{\mathrm{-gen}}}
. We only need to show that
\iota_{P}^{!}\circ h_{l,*}({\mathcal{F}}\widetilde{\boxtimes}{\mathcal{M}})
is in the essential image of the pullback functor
\DMod(\Bun_{M})\to\DMod(\Bun_{P})
, where
M
is the Levi quotient group of
P
.

The fiber product

\Bun_{P}\mathbin{\mathop{\times}\displaylimits_{\Bun_{G}^{P{\mathrm{-gen}}},h_{l}}}\Hecke_{G,x}^{P{\mathrm{-gen}}}

classifies
({\mathcal{P}}_{P}^{l},{\mathcal{P}}_{G}^{r},\delta)
, where
{\mathcal{P}}_{P}^{l}
(resp.
{\mathcal{P}}_{G}^{r}
) is a
P
-torsor (
G
-torsor) on
X
, and
\delta:G\mathbin{\mathop{\times}\displaylimits^{P}}{\mathcal{P}}_{P}^{l}|_{X\setminus x}\to{\mathcal{P}}_{G}^{r}|_{X\setminus x}
is an isomorphism. In other words, we have

\Bun_{P}\mathbin{\mathop{\times}\displaylimits_{\Bun_{G}^{P{\mathrm{-gen}}},h_{l}}}\Hecke_{G,x}^{P{\mathrm{-gen}}}\simeq\Bun_{P}\mathbin{\mathop{\times}\displaylimits_{\Bun_{G},h_{l}}}\Hecke_{G,x}

Hence we have the following Cartesian square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Bun_{P}\mathbin{\mathop{\times}\displaylimits_{\Bun_{G},h_{l}}}\Hecke_{G,x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hecke_{G,x}^{P{\mathrm{-gen}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Bun_{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Bun_{G}^{P{\mathrm{-gen}}}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{pr}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{l}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{P}}

Using the base-change isomorphism, we obtain

\iota_{P}^{!}\circ h_{l,*}({\mathcal{F}}\widetilde{\boxtimes}{\mathcal{M}})\simeq\mathrm{pr}_{1,*}\circ v^{!}({\mathcal{F}}\widetilde{\boxtimes}{\mathcal{M}}).

Note that we also have

\Bun_{P}\mathbin{\mathop{\times}\displaylimits_{\Bun_{G},h_{l}}}\Hecke_{G,x}\simeq\Bun_{P}\widetilde{\mathbin{\mathop{\times}\displaylimits}}\Gr_{G,x},\;\Hecke_{P,x}\simeq\Bun_{P}\widetilde{\mathbin{\mathop{\times}\displaylimits}}\Gr_{P,x}.

It is well-known that
\Gr_{P,x}\to\Gr_{G,x}
is a stratification (see e.g.
[
Che20b
, Appendix C.3]
), hence so is the map

u:\Hecke_{P,x}\to\Bun_{P}\mathbin{\mathop{\times}\displaylimits_{\Bun_{G},h_{l}}}\Hecke_{G,x}.

Hence it remains to show that
\mathrm{pr}_{1,*}\circ u_{*}\circ u^{!}\circ v^{!}({\mathcal{F}}\widetilde{\boxtimes}{\mathcal{M}})
is contained in the essential image of the pullback functor
\DMod(\Bun_{M})\to\DMod(\Bun_{P})
. Note that we can write
\Hecke_{P,x}
as

\Hecke_{P,x}\simeq\Gr_{P,x}\widetilde{\mathbin{\mathop{\times}\displaylimits}}\Bun_{P}.

Thanks to this, we obtain

\mathrm{pr}_{1,*}\circ u_{*}\circ u^{!}\circ v^{!}({\mathcal{F}}\widetilde{\boxtimes}{\mathcal{M}})\simeq h_{l,*}(w^{!}({\mathcal{F}})\widetilde{\boxtimes}\iota_{P}^{!}({\mathcal{M}})),

where
w^{!}({\mathcal{F}})\in\Sph_{P,x}
is the pullback of
{\mathcal{F}}
along
w:P({\mathcal{O}}_{x})\backslash P({\mathcal{K}}_{x})/P({\mathcal{O}}_{x})\to G({\mathcal{O}}_{x})\backslash G({\mathcal{K}}_{x})/G({\mathcal{O}}_{x})
and
h_{l}:\Hecke_{P,x}\to\Bun_{P}
is the left projection map. In other words, the RHS is just
w^{!}({\mathcal{F}})\mathbin{\mathop{\star}\displaylimits_{\Sph_{P,x}}}\iota_{P}^{!}({\mathcal{M}})
. By assumption,
\iota_{P}^{!}({\mathcal{M}})\simeq q^{!}({\mathcal{N}})
for some
{\mathcal{N}}\in\DMod(\Bun_{M})
, where
q:\Bun_{P}\to\Bun_{M}
is the projection. Now Lemma
2.4.1
below implies
w^{!}({\mathcal{F}})\mathbin{\mathop{\star}\displaylimits_{\Sph_{P,x}}}\iota_{P}^{!}({\mathcal{M}})
is contained in the essential image of
q^{!}
as desired.

∎[Proposition
2.3.15
]

The following lemma was used above:

Lemma 2.4.1
.

For any parabolic subgroup
P
of
G
and its Levi quotient group
M
, the
\Sph_{P,x}
-action on
\DMod(\Bun_{P})
preserves the essential image of the functor
q^{!}:\DMod(\Bun_{M})\to\DMod(\Bun_{P})
.

Proof.

Consider the maps
\mathrm{pr}_{1},\mathrm{pr}_{2}:\Bun_{P}\mathbin{\mathop{\times}\displaylimits_{\Bun_{M}}}\Bun_{P}\to\Bun_{P}
. Since
q
is universally homologically contractible, the functors
\mathrm{pr}_{1}^{!},\mathrm{pr}_{2}^{!}
are fully faithful. By smooth descent for D-modules, an object
{\mathcal{K}}\in\DMod(\Bun_{P})
is in the essential image of
q^{!}
iff there exists an equivalence
\mathrm{pr}_{1}^{!}({\mathcal{K}})\simeq\mathrm{pr}_{2}^{!}({\mathcal{K}})
.

Let
y\neq x
be another closed point of
X
. Consider the fiber product

H:=\Hecke_{P,y}\mathbin{\mathop{\times}\displaylimits_{\Hecke_{M,y}}}\Bun_{M}

classifying Hecke modifications of
P
-torsors at
y
whose induced modifications of
M
-torsors are trivial. By Lemma
2.4.2
below, the natural map

\theta:H\to\Bun_{P}\mathbin{\mathop{\times}\displaylimits_{\Bun_{M}}}\Bun_{P}

is universally homologically contractible.

Combining the above two paragraphs, we see an object
{\mathcal{K}}\in\DMod(\Bun_{P})
is in the essential image of
q^{!}
iff there exists an equivalence
(\mathrm{pr}_{1}\circ\theta)^{!}({\mathcal{K}})\simeq(\mathrm{pr}_{2}\circ\theta)^{!}({\mathcal{K}})
.

Now let
{\mathcal{F}}\in\Sph_{P,x}
and
{\mathcal{M}}=q^{!}({\mathcal{N}})\in\DMod(\Bun_{P})
. Using the assumption
y\neq x
, it is easy to see

H\mathbin{\mathop{\times}\displaylimits_{\mathrm{pr}_{1}\circ\theta,\Bun_{P},h_{l}}}\Hecke_{P,x}\simeq H\mathbin{\mathop{\times}\displaylimits_{\mathrm{pr}_{2}\circ\theta,\Bun_{P},h_{l}}}\Hecke_{P,x}\simeq\Hecke_{P,x\cup y}\mathbin{\mathop{\times}\displaylimits_{\Hecke_{M,y}}}\Bun_{M}.

This space classifies Hecke modifications of
P
-torsors at
x\cup y
whose induced modifications of
M
-torsors at
y
are trivial. Thus, for
i=1
or
2
, we have maps

v_{i}:H^{\prime}:=\Hecke_{P,x\cup y}\mathbin{\mathop{\times}\displaylimits_{\Hecke_{M,y}}}\Bun_{M}\to\Hecke_{P,x}

and Cartesian diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hecke_{P,x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Bun_{P}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{pr}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{l}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{pr}_{i}\circ\theta}

Using the base-change isomorphisms, it remains to show that

v_{1}^{!}({\mathcal{F}}\widetilde{\boxtimes}q^{!}{{\mathcal{N}}})\simeq v_{2}^{!}({\mathcal{F}}\widetilde{\boxtimes}q^{!}{{\mathcal{N}}}).

But this follows from the fact that the maps

H^{\prime}\xrightarrow{v_{i}}\Hecke_{P,x}\to P({\mathcal{O}}_{x})\backslash P({\mathcal{K}}_{x})/P({\mathcal{O}}_{x}),\;H^{\prime}\xrightarrow{v_{i}}\Hecke_{P,x}\xrightarrow{h_{r}}\Bun_{P}\xrightarrow{q}\Bun_{M}

are independent of
i
.

∎[Lemma
2.4.1
]

The following lemma was used above:

Lemma 2.4.2
.

For any closed point
y\in X
, the natural map

\theta:\Hecke_{P,y}\mathbin{\mathop{\times}\displaylimits_{\Hecke_{M,y}}}\Bun_{M}\to\Bun_{P}\mathbin{\mathop{\times}\displaylimits_{\Bun_{M}}}\Bun_{P}

is universally homologically contractible.

Proof.

For any finite type affine test scheme
s:S\to\Bun_{P}\mathbin{\mathop{\times}\displaylimits_{\Bun_{M}}}\Bun_{P}
, we need to show that base-change
\theta^{\prime}:S^{\prime}\to S
of
\theta
along
s
is homologically contractible, i.e.,
(\theta^{\prime})^{!}
is fully faithful. By definition,
s
corresponds to
({\mathcal{F}}_{P}^{l},{\mathcal{F}}_{P}^{r},\alpha)
where
{\mathcal{F}}_{P}^{l}
and
{\mathcal{F}}_{P}^{r}
are
P
-torsors on
X\times S
and
\alpha
is an isomorphism between their induced
M
-torsors. Then, for any finite type affine test
S
-scheme
T
, the groupoid
\Maps_{S}(T,S^{\prime})
, which is actually a set, classifies isomorphisms

\beta:{\mathcal{F}}_{P}^{l}|_{(X\setminus y)\times T}\to{\mathcal{F}}_{P}^{r}|_{(X\setminus y)\times T}

between
P
-torsors on
(X\setminus x)\times T
such that the induced isomorphism

M\mathbin{\mathop{\times}\displaylimits^{P}}\beta:M\mathbin{\mathop{\times}\displaylimits^{P}}{\mathcal{F}}_{P}^{l}|_{(X\setminus y)\times T}\to M\mathbin{\mathop{\times}\displaylimits^{P}}{\mathcal{F}}_{P}^{r}|_{(X\setminus y)\times T}

is the base-change of
\alpha
along
(X\setminus y)\times T\to X\mathbin{\mathop{\times}\displaylimits}S
.

We first show that
\Maps_{S}(T,S^{\prime})
is non-empty. To this end, we can assume
T=S
. Since
(X\setminus y)\times S
is affine, any
P
-torsor on it has an
M
-reduction. Choose
M
-reductions
{\mathcal{F}}_{M}^{l}
and
{\mathcal{F}}_{M}^{r}
of
{\mathcal{F}}_{P}^{l}|_{(X\setminus y)\times S}
and
{\mathcal{F}}_{P}^{r}|_{(X\setminus y)\times S}
. Then we have

M\mathbin{\mathop{\times}\displaylimits^{P}}{\mathcal{F}}_{P}^{l}|_{(X\setminus y)\times S}\simeq M\mathbin{\mathop{\times}\displaylimits^{P}}P\mathbin{\mathop{\times}\displaylimits^{M}}{\mathcal{F}}_{M}^{l}\simeq{\mathcal{F}}_{M}^{l}

and similar isomorphisms for
{\mathcal{F}}_{M}^{r}
. Hence the base-change of
\alpha
induces an isomorphism between
{\mathcal{F}}_{M}^{l}
and
{\mathcal{F}}_{M}^{r}
, which in turn yields an element in
\Maps_{S}(S,S^{\prime})
.

Let
H_{T}
be the (abstract) group of those automorphisms of the
P
-torsor
{\mathcal{F}}_{P}^{l}|_{(X\setminus y)\times T}
such that the induced automorphism of
M\mathbin{\mathop{\times}\displaylimits^{P}}{\mathcal{F}}_{P}^{l}|_{(X\setminus y)\times T}
is the identity map. It follows that
\Maps_{S}(T,S^{\prime})
is a non-empty
H_{T}
-torsor. On the other hand, using the
M
-reductions
{\mathcal{F}}_{M}^{l}
of
{\mathcal{F}}_{P}^{l}|_{(X\setminus y)\times S}
, we see that
H_{T}
can be identified with the group of
M
-equivariant maps
{\mathcal{F}}_{M}^{l}|_{(X\setminus y)\times T}\to U
. Choose a
{\mathbb{G}}_{M}
-action on
G
that contracts
U
into the unit element and fixes
M
. Using this
{\mathbb{G}}_{m}
-action, we see that
T\mapsto H_{T}
is represented by a contractible group prestack over
S
. It follows that
T\mapsto\Maps_{S}(T,S^{\prime})
is represented by a contractible prestack over
S
. By definition, this prestack is
S^{\prime}
.

∎[Lemma
2.4.2
]

3.
Tempered objects in the parabolic category
{\mathcal{I}}(G,P)

Throughout this section, we fix a parabolic subgroup
P
of
G
. Let
U
be its unipotent radical and
M=P/U
be its Levi quotient group. We fix a splitting
M\to P
and view
M
also as a Levi subgroup.

We fix a closed point
x\in X
and an identification
{\mathcal{O}}_{x}\simeq{\mathcal{O}}
, which provide an equivalence
\Sph_{G}\simeq\Sph_{G,x}
. We use this equivalence to define
G
-tempered objects in
\DMod(\Bun_{G})
and
{\mathcal{I}}(G,P)
(see Example
2.1.8
and Proposition
2.3.15
).

The goal of this section is to prove the following theorems:

Theorem 3.0.1
.

We have:

(1)

The functor
\CT_{P,*}:\DMod(\Bun_{G})\to\DMod(\Bun_{M})
sends
G
-tempered objects to
M
-tempered objects.

(2)

The functor
\Eis_{P,!}:\DMod(\Bun_{M})\to\DMod(\Bun_{G})
sends
M
-anti-tempered objects to
G
-anti-tempered objects.

(3)

The functor
\Eis_{P,*}:\DMod(\Bun_{M})\to\DMod(\Bun_{G})
sends
M
-tempered objects to
G
-tempered objects.

Theorem 3.0.2
.

We have:

(1)

The functor
\iota_{M}^{!}:{\mathcal{I}}(G,P)\to\DMod(\Bun_{M})
sends
G
-tempered objects to
M
-tempered objects.

(2)

The functor
\iota_{M,!}:\DMod(\Bun_{M})\to{\mathcal{I}}(G,P)
sends
M
-anti-tempered objects to
G
-anti-tempered objects.

(3)

The functor
\iota_{M,*}:\DMod(\Bun_{M})\to{\mathcal{I}}(G,P)
sends
M
-tempered objects to
G
-tempered objects.

(4)

The functor
\iota_{M}^{*}:{\mathcal{I}}(G,P)\to\DMod(\Bun_{M})
sends
G
-anti-tempered objects to
M
-anti-tempered objects.

Theorem
3.0.1
can be deduced from Theorem
3.0.2
as follows.

Proof of Theorem
3.0.1
.

Recall the functors

\Eis_{P\to G}^{\mathrm{enh}}:{\mathcal{I}}(G,P)\adjoint\DMod(\Bun_{G}):\CT_{G\leftarrow P}^{\mathrm{enh}}

are
\Sph_{G,x}
-linear. Hence by Remark
2.3.19
, we see Theorem
3.0.1
(1)-(3) follow respectively from Theorem
3.0.2
(1)-(3).

∎[Theorem
3.0.1
]

Our strategy for Theorem
3.0.2
is to deduce it from its local analogue. This is done as follows.

In §
3.1
, we recall the local analogue of
{\mathcal{I}}(G,P)
, known as the
semi-infinite category

\SI_{P}:=\DMod(\Gr_{G})^{U({\mathcal{K}})M({\mathcal{O}})}.

In §
3.2
-
3.4
, we state and prove the local analogue of Theorem
3.0.2
(see Theorem
3.2.1
).

In §
3.2
, we reduce this local result to proving that a certain hyperbolic localization functor
\HL_{P}^{!}
(resp.
\HL_{P}^{*}
):
\Sph_{G}\to\Sph_{M}
preserves temperedness (resp. detects anti-temperedness), see Proposition
3.2.5
and Proposition
3.2.6
. The main ingredient for this reduction is the duality between
\SI_{P}
and
\SI_{P^{-}}
worked out in
[
Che20b
]
,
[
Che21
]
.

In §
3.3
, we prove the claim about
\HL_{P}^{!}
. The main ingredients in its proof include: (i) V. Lafforgue’s equivalence
\Sph_{G}\simeq\DMod(\Bun_{G}({\mathbb{P}}^{1}))
, see
[
Laf09
]
and also
[
Ber21c
, §3.1]
; (ii) the compatibility between
\HL_{P}^{!}
and
\CT_{P,*}
via Lafforgue’s equivalence (Proposition
3.3.2
); (ii) the explicit description of tempered objects in
\DMod(\Bun_{G}({\mathbb{P}}^{1}))
in
[
Ber21b
, §3.5]
.

In §
3.4
, we prove the claim about
\HL_{P}^{*}
. It can be easily reduced to the case
P=B
, and in that case it follows from the well-known fact (see
[
MV07
]
) that the hyperbolic localization functor
\HL_{B}^{*}
sends the heart of
\Sph_{G}
to the heart of
\Sph_{T}
up to a cohomological shift.

In §
3.5
, we deduce Theorem
3.0.2
from its local analogue. The main ingredient is a factorization of the functor
\iota_{M,!}
as

\DMod(\Bun_{M})\to\SI_{P,x}\mathbin{\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits_{\Sph_{M,x}}}\DMod(\Bun_{M})\to{\mathcal{I}}(G,P)

which relates the local and global picture.

In §
3.6
, we provide a (conjectural) explanation of the results about
\SI_{P}
on the spectral side of the Langlands duality.

3.1.
Recollection: the semi-infinite category
\SI_{P}

In this section, we recall the semi-infinite category

\SI_{P}:=\DMod(\Gr_{G})^{U({\mathcal{K}})M({\mathcal{O}})},

which is the
U({\mathcal{K}})M({\mathcal{O}})
-invariant category of
\DMod(\Gr_{G})
(See
[
Ras16
, §2]
or
[
Che20b
, §1.4]
). It carries commuting actions of the spherical categories
\Sph_{G}
and
\Sph_{M}
. Namely, if we realize
\Gr_{G}
as
G({\mathcal{O}})\backslash G({\mathcal{K}})
, then the
\Sph_{G}
-action is induced by convolution from the left, while the
\Sph_{M}
-action is induced by the right
M({\mathcal{K}})
-action on
G({\mathcal{O}})\backslash G({\mathcal{K}})
.

As mentioned at the beginning of this section,
\SI_{P}
should be viewed as a local analogue of
{\mathcal{I}}(G,P)
. Let us describe the local analogue of the adjoint functors

\displaystyle\iota_{M,!}:

\displaystyle\DMod(\Bun_{M})\adjoint{\mathcal{I}}(G,P)

\displaystyle:\iota_{M}^{!},

\displaystyle\iota_{M}^{*}:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{I}}(G,P)\adjoint\DMod(\Bun_{M})

\displaystyle:\iota_{M,*}.

Construction 3.1.1
.

We have adjoint functors (See
[
Che20b
, Lemma 2.3.4]
)

\displaystyle\iota_{P,!}:\DMod(\Gr_{P})^{U({\mathcal{K}})}\adjoint\DMod(\Gr_{G})^{U({\mathcal{K}})}:\iota_{P}^{!},

\displaystyle\iota_{P}^{*}:\DMod(\Gr_{G})^{U({\mathcal{K}})}\adjoint\DMod(\Gr_{P})^{U({\mathcal{K}})}:\iota_{P,*}

induced by pushforwards and pullbacks along
3
3

3

In §
2.3
, we wrote the map
\Bun_{P}\to\Bun_{G}^{P{\mathrm{-gen}}}
using the same notation
\iota_{P}
. We will articulate the source and target of the maps if there is danger of ambiguity. In any case, the map
\Gr_{P}\to\Gr_{G}
should be viewed as a local analogue of
\Bun_{P}\to\Bun_{G}^{P{\mathrm{-gen}}}
.

\iota_{P}:\Gr_{P}\to\Gr_{G}
.

The functors
\iota_{P}^{!}
and
\iota_{P,*}
are obviously
M({\mathcal{K}})
-linear; by
[
Gai20
, Lemma D.4.4]
, so are their left adjoints. Hence, the above adjoint functors induce
\Sph_{M}
-linear adjoint functors on
M({\mathcal{O}})
-invariants, i.e., between
\SI_{P}
and
\DMod(\Gr_{P})^{U({\mathcal{K}})M({\mathcal{O}})}
.

On the other hand, the
!
-pullback functor along
\Gr_{P}\to\Gr_{M}
induces equivalences (see
[
Che20b
, Lemma 2.3.2]
):

\DMod(\Gr_{M})\simeq\DMod(\Gr_{P})^{U({\mathcal{K}})},\;\Sph_{M}\simeq\DMod(\Gr_{P})^{U({\mathcal{K}})M({\mathcal{O}})}.

Combining with the above paragraph, we obtain
\Sph_{M}
-linear adjoint functors:

\displaystyle\iota_{M,!}:\Sph_{M}\adjoint\SI_{P}:\iota_{M}^{!},\;\;\;\iota_{M}^{*}:\SI_{P}\adjoint\Sph_{M}:\iota_{M,*}

It is well-known that the connected components of
\Gr_{P}
provide a stratification on
\Gr_{G}
(see
[
Che20b
, §C.3]
). It follows that
\iota_{M}^{!}
is conservative and that
\SI_{P}
is compactly generated by
\iota_{M,!}({\mathcal{F}})
for compact objects
{\mathcal{F}}\in\Sph_{M}
. Also,
\iota_{M,*}
(resp.
\iota^{*}
) is the unique continuous right inverse of
\iota_{M}^{!}
(resp. left inverse of
\iota_{M,!}
).

Theorem 3.1.2

(​​
[
Che21
, Theorem A, Theorem C]
)
.

Let
P^{-}
be any parabolic subgroup of
G
opposite to
P
and
U^{-}
be its unipotent radical. Then the categories
\DMod(\Gr_{G})^{U({\mathcal{K}})}
and
\DMod(\Gr_{G})^{U^{-}({\mathcal{K}})}
are dual to each other, with the pairing functor given by

\DMod(\Gr_{G})^{U({\mathcal{K}})}\mathbin{\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits}\DMod(\Gr_{G})^{U^{-}({\mathcal{K}})}\xrightarrow{\oblv}\DMod(\Gr_{G})\mathbin{\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits}\DMod(\Gr_{G})\to\mathrm{Vect},

where the rightmost arrow is the Verdier self-pairing on
\Gr_{G}
. Moreover, via this duality and the Verdier self-duality on
\Gr_{M}
, the functors

\displaystyle\DMod(\Gr_{M})

\displaystyle\simeq\DMod(\Gr_{P})^{U({\mathcal{K}})}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\iota_{P,!}}\DMod(\Gr_{G})^{U({\mathcal{K}})},

\displaystyle\DMod(\Gr_{M})

\displaystyle\simeq\DMod(\Gr_{P^{-}})^{U^{-}({\mathcal{K}})}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\iota_{P^{-},!}}\DMod(\Gr_{G})^{U^{-}({\mathcal{K}})}

are conjugate to each other.

One can easily check that the Verdier self-pairing
\DMod(\Gr_{G})\mathbin{\mathop{\otimes}\displaylimits}\DMod(\Gr_{G})\to\mathrm{Vect}
is compatible with the
\Sph_{G}
-action on
\DMod(\Gr_{G})
. It follows that the duality in Theorem
3.1.2
is compatible with the
\Sph_{G}
-actions on
\DMod(\Gr_{G})^{U({\mathcal{K}})}
and
\DMod(\Gr_{G})^{U^{-}({\mathcal{K}})}
. Similarly, the Verdier self-pairing on
\Gr_{G}
is compatible with the
{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{K}})
-action. It follows we can take
{\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{O}})
-invariants and obtain the following result.

Corollary 3.1.3

(C.f.
[
Che20b
, Corollary 1.4.5]
)
.

Let
P^{-}
be any parabolic subgroup of
G
opposite to
P
. Then the categories
\SI_{P}
and
\SI_{P^{-}}
are dual to each other. This duality is compatible with the
\Sph_{M}
-actions and
\Sph_{G}
-actions. Moreover, via this duality and the Verdier self-duality of
\Sph_{M}
, the functors

\displaystyle\iota_{M,!}:\Sph_{M}\to\SI_{P},\;\;\iota_{M,!}^{-}:\Sph_{M}\to\SI_{P^{-}}

are conjugate to each other.

Corollary 3.1.4
.

Via the above duality between
\SI_{P}
and
\SI_{P^{-}}
, and the Verdier self-duality of
\Sph_{M}
, we have:

(1)

The functors

\displaystyle\iota_{M}^{!}:\SI_{P}\to\Sph_{M},\;\;\iota_{M,!}^{-}:\Sph_{M}\to\SI_{P^{-}}

are dual to each other.

(2)

The functors

\displaystyle\iota_{M}^{*}:\SI_{P}\to\Sph_{M},\;\;\iota_{M,*}^{-}:\Sph_{M}\to\SI_{P^{-}}

are dual to each other.

Proof.

(1) follows from the formal fact that the conjugate functor is left adjoint to the dual functor (see e.g.
[
Gai16
, §1.5]
). (2) follows from the fact that
\iota_{M,*}
(resp.
\iota^{*}
) is the unique right inverse of
\iota_{M}^{!}
(resp. left inverse of
\iota_{M,!}
).

∎[Corollary
3.1.4
]

The last topic of this subsection is to describe the local analogue of the functors

\Eis^{\mathrm{enh}}_{P\to G}:{\mathcal{I}}(G,P)\adjoint\DMod(\Bun_{G}):\CT_{G\leftarrow P}^{\mathrm{enh}}.

Construction 3.1.5
.

We have adjoint functors

\Av_{!}^{U({\mathcal{K}})/U({\mathcal{O}})}:\Sph_{G}\adjoint\SI_{P}:\Av_{*}^{G({\mathcal{O}})/P({\mathcal{O}})}.

More precisely, the right adjoint is the composition

\SI_{P}:=\DMod(\Gr_{G})^{U({\mathcal{K}})M({\mathcal{O}})}\xrightarrow{\oblv}\DMod(\Gr_{G})^{P({\mathcal{O}})}\xrightarrow{\Av_{*}}\DMod(\Gr_{G})^{G({\mathcal{O}})}=:\Sph_{G}.

Its left adjoint is well-defined and equivalent to

-\mathbin{\mathop{\star}\displaylimits_{\Sph_{G}}}\Delta_{0}:\Sph_{G}\to\SI_{P},

where
\Delta_{0}:=\iota_{M,!}(\mathbf{1}_{\Sph_{M}})\in\SI_{P}
is the
0
-standard object. Note that both functors are
\Sph_{G}
-linear by Appendix
A
.

The readers might object the above analogy because the functor
\Eis^{\mathrm{enh}}_{P\to G}:{\mathcal{I}}(G,P)\to\DMod(\Bun_{G})
is a left adjoint, while the functor
\Av_{*}^{G({\mathcal{O}})/P({\mathcal{O}})}:\SI_{P}\to\Sph_{G}
is a right adjoint. But by Corollary
3.1.8
below,
\Av_{*}^{G({\mathcal{O}})/P({\mathcal{O}})}
is also left adjoint to
\Av_{!}^{U({\mathcal{K}})/U({\mathcal{O}})}
up to a cohomological shift. To prove this, we need the following result:

Lemma 3.1.6

(C.f.
[
Ras16
, Corollary 6.2.3]
)
.

Consider the parahoric subgroup
Ph_{P}:=G({\mathcal{O}})\mathbin{\mathop{\times}\displaylimits_{G}}P
of
G({\mathcal{K}})
. For any
{\mathcal{C}}
equipped with a strong
G({\mathcal{K}})
-action, the composition

{\mathcal{C}}^{U({\mathcal{K}})M({\mathcal{O}})}\xrightarrow{\oblv}{\mathcal{C}}^{P({\mathcal{O}})}\xrightarrow{\Av_{*}}{\mathcal{C}}^{Ph_{P}}

is an equivalence.

Proof.

If
P=B
, then
Ph_{P}
is the Iwahori subgroup of
G
and the lemma is just
[
Ras16
, Corollary 6.2.3]
. The general case can be proved in exactly the same way.

∎[Lemma
3.1.6
]

In particular, we have a pair of inverse functors

\Av_{!}^{U({\mathcal{K}})/U({\mathcal{O}})}:\DMod(\Gr_{G})^{Ph_{P}}\adjoint\SI_{P}:\Av_{*}^{Ph_{P}/P({\mathcal{O}})}.

Again, both functors are
\Sph_{G}
-linear by Appendix
A
.

Lemma 3.1.7
.

The composition

\Sph_{G}\xrightarrow{\Av_{!}^{U({\mathcal{K}})/U({\mathcal{O}})}}\SI_{P}\xrightarrow{\Av_{*}^{Ph_{P}/P({\mathcal{O}})}}\DMod(\Gr_{G})^{Ph_{P}}

is equivalent to the forgetful functor
\oblv^{G({\mathcal{O}})\to Ph_{P}}
.

Proof.

Since both functors are
\Sph_{G}
-linear, we only need to show they send the unit
\mathbf{1}_{\Sph_{G}}
to isomorphic objects, i.e.,

\Av_{*}^{Ph_{P}/P({\mathcal{O}})}(\Delta_{0})\simeq\oblv^{G({\mathcal{O}})\to Ph_{P}}(\mathbf{1}_{\Sph_{G}}).

Applying
\Av_{!}^{U({\mathcal{K}})/U({\mathcal{O}})}
, we only need to show

\Delta_{0}\simeq\Av_{!}^{U({\mathcal{K}})/U({\mathcal{O}})}\circ\oblv^{G({\mathcal{O}})\to Ph_{P}}(\mathbf{1}_{\Sph_{G}}).

But this is obvious.

∎[Lemma
3.1.7
]

Corollary 3.1.8
.

We have adjoint functors

\Av_{*}^{G({\mathcal{O}})/P({\mathcal{O}})}[2\dim(G/P)]:\SI_{P}\adjoint\Sph_{G}:\Av_{!}^{U({\mathcal{K}})/U({\mathcal{O}})}.

In other words, the left adjoint and right adjoint of
\Av_{!}^{U({\mathcal{K}})/U({\mathcal{O}})}
are equivalent up to a cohomological shift.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.1.7
, it suffices to prove that the left adjoint and right adjoint of
\oblv^{G({\mathcal{O}})\to Ph_{P}}:\Sph_{G}\to\DMod(\Gr_{G})^{Ph_{P}}
are equivalent up to the cohomological shift
[2\dim(G/P)]
. This follows from the fact that
G({\mathcal{O}})/Ph_{P}\simeq G/P
is smooth and proper.

∎[Corollary
3.1.8
]

3.2.
Tempered objects in the semi-infinite category
\SI_{P}

In this subsection, we prove the following local analogue of Theorem
3.0.2
. See also §
3.6
for an explanation of its counterpart on the spectral side.

Theorem 3.2.1
.

We have

(1)

The functors
\iota_{M}^{!}:\SI_{P}\to\Sph_{M}
sends
G
-tempered objects to
M
-tempered objects.

(2)

The functor
\iota_{M,!}:\Sph_{M}\to\SI_{P}
sends
M
-anti-tempered objects to
G
-anti-tempered objects.

(3)

The functor
\iota_{M,*}:\Sph_{M}\to\SI_{P}
sends
M
-tempered objects to
G
-tempered objects.

(4)

The functor
\iota_{M}^{*}:\SI_{P}\to\Sph_{M}
sends
G
-anti-tempered objects to
M
-anti-tempered objects.

Let us first mention the following corollary of the theorem:

Corollary 3.2.2
.

For any parabolic subgroup
P
of
G
, any
G
-tempered object in
\SI_{P}
is
M
-tempered.

Proof.

Follows from Theorem
3.2.1
(1) and the fact that
\iota_{M}^{!}
is conservative and
\Sph_{M}
-linear.

∎[Corollary
3.2.2
]

Remark 3.2.3
.

We will also prove the global analogue of the corollary (see Proposition
4.2.10
and remark below it) once we define
M
-tempered objects (or in fact
P
-tempered
objects) in
{\mathcal{I}}(G,P)
. Such definition is
not
obvious because there is no
\Sph_{M}
-action on
{\mathcal{I}}(G,P)
.

Proof of Theorem
3.2.1
.

The following lemma can be easily proven by unwinding definitions:

Lemma 3.2.4
.

Let
{\mathcal{C}}
and
{\mathcal{D}}
be
\Sph_{G}
-module categories. Suppose
{\mathcal{C}}
and
{\mathcal{D}}
are dual to each other and the duality is compatible with the
\Sph_{G}
-actions. Then

•

{\mathcal{C}}^{G\mathrm{-temp}}
and
{\mathcal{D}}^{G\mathrm{-}\mathrm{temp}}
are dual to each other, and the functors

\oblv:{\mathcal{C}}^{G\mathrm{-temp}}\to{\mathcal{C}},\;\;\mathrm{temp}_{G}:{\mathcal{D}}\to{\mathcal{D}}^{G\mathrm{-temp}}

are dual to each other;

•

{\mathcal{C}}^{G\mathrm{-atemp}}
and
{\mathcal{D}}^{G\mathrm{-}\mathrm{atemp}}
are dual to each other, and the functors

\oblv:{\mathcal{C}}^{G\mathrm{-atemp}}\to{\mathcal{C}},\;\;\mathrm{atemp}_{G}:{\mathcal{D}}\to{\mathcal{D}}^{G\mathrm{-atemp}}

are dual to each other.

Let
P^{-}
be any parabolic subgroup of
G
opposite to
P
. By the above lemma and Corollary
3.1.4
, the following statements are equivalent:

•

The functor
